<!-- mainthm-1: body_md -->
Let $0<\beta\le 1$ and $\varrho>0$. For every $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ and $t\ge0$,
$$
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\le \min\bigl\{1,2^{d/2}e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\bigr\}
\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi},
$$
$S_\beta$ leaves $M_\Phi$ invariant, and it is a $C_0$-semigroup on
$M_\Phi$ whenever $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$. For compatible Young's functions $\Phi$ and
$\Psi$ there is $C=C(d,\beta,b)$, independent of $\Phi,\Psi,\Theta$ and
$\varrho$, such that
$$
\bigl\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\bigr\|_{L_\Psi}
\ \le\ C\,
\frac{\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)}
     {\Psi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)}\,
\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi},\qquad t>0 ,
$$
and in particular
$\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\le C\,\Phi^{-1}(t^{-d/(2\beta)})
\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}$. For every $\theta\in(0,1)$, both estimates may be
multiplied by $C_\theta e^{-(1-\theta)\lambda_\beta t}$, and the rate
$\lambda_\beta$ cannot be improved.
<!-- end mainthm-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\rm[Sharpness of the large-time rate]\label{rem:sharp-intro}

For $\Phi=\Psi$, the exponential rate in Theorem~A is optimal in a strong
sense. Let $\omega=\sqrt\varrho$ and let $h_0(x)=e^{-\omega|x|^2/2}$ be the
ground state of $\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}$, so that $\mathcal{H}_{\varrho} h_0=d\omega h_0$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}(iv), $S_\beta(t)h_0=e^{-\lambda_\beta t}h_0$, and
therefore
$$
\|S_\beta(t)h_0\|_{L_\Phi}=e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\|h_0\|_{L_\Phi},
\qquad t\ge0,\ \Phi\in\mathcal{Y} ,
$$
where $0<\|h_0\|_{L_\Phi}<\infty$ (see the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:OrliczSmoothing}(a)(iv)). We deduce that
$$
e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\ \le\ \|S_\beta(t)\|_{L_\Phi\to L_\Phi}
\ \le\ \min\bigl\{1,2^{d/2}e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\bigr\},
\qquad t\ge0 ,
$$
so that the operator norm is determined up to the factor $2^{d/2}$. On
$L^2$, the spectral theorem gives $\|S_\beta(t)\|_{L^2\to L^2}=e^{-\lambda_\beta t}$
exactly. For short times, the profile $\Phi^{-1}(t^{-d/(2\beta)})$ is sharp in
the case $\beta=1$, $\Phi(x)=x$ (that is, from $L^1$ to $L^\infty$), by the
computation of $\mathcal{K}_t(0,0)$ above. We do not address the optimality of the
short-time factor for general pairs of Orlicz spaces.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- mainthm-2: body_md -->
Let $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ and let $f$ be locally Lipschitz with $f(0)=0$. Assume that
there exists $\lambda>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IC-infty}
\int_1^\infty x^{-p_\ast}\,\ell\bigl(\lambda\,\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x<\infty .
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{eq:main} is locally well posed in $M_\Phi$: solutions exist on a
common interval for data in compact subsets of $M_\Phi$, they are unique in
the class of bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solutions, they depend continuously on the
data, and they are ordered if $f$ is increasing. If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IC-zero}
\int_0^\infty x^{-p_\ast}\,\ell\bigl(\lambda\,\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x
<\frac{d}{2\beta},
\end{equation}
then small data give global solutions. Finally, if \eqref{eq:IC-infty}
holds and $\int_0^1\ell(r)\,\,\mathrm{d} r/r<\infty$, then small data give global
solutions which decay like $e^{-\lambda_\beta t}$ in $L^\infty$ and in
$L_\Phi$.
<!-- end mainthm-2 -->
<!-- mainthm-3: body_md -->
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm{C})$, $(\Lambda)$ and $(\mathrm{G})$. If
$\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ and $\Phi\gtrsim f$ at infinity, then \eqref{eq:main} is locally
well posed in $M_\Phi$. Conversely, if $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ and $\Phi\lesssim f$ at
infinity, then there exists a nonnegative $u_0\in L_\Phi$ for which
\eqref{eq:main} admits no nonnegative integral solution on $\mathbb R^d\times(0,T)$
for any $T>0$.
<!-- end mainthm-3 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:orlicz}
A function $\Phi:[0,\infty]\to[0,\infty]$ with $\Phi(0)=0$ is a
\emph{Young's function} if one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\Roman*)}]
\item $\Phi$ is convex, increasing, finite-valued and not identically zero on
$[0,\infty)$;
\item there exists $x_\infty^\Phi>0$ such that $\Phi$ is convex, increasing
and finite-valued on $[0,x_\infty^\Phi]$ and $\Phi(x)=\infty$ for
$x>x_\infty^\Phi$;
\item there exists $x_\infty^\Phi>0$ such that $\Phi$ is convex, increasing
and finite-valued on $[0,x_\infty^\Phi)$, $\lim_{x\uparrow
x_\infty^\Phi}\Phi(x)=\infty$ and $\Phi(x)=\infty$ for $x\ge x_\infty^\Phi$.
\end{enumerate}
The set of Young's functions is denoted by $\mathcal{Y}$. We say that $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ is
\emph{finite} if it satisfies \textup{(I)}, and \emph{positive} if $\Phi>0$ on
$(0,\infty)$.

The \emph{Orlicz space} $L_\Phi$ is the set of measurable $u:\mathbb R^d\to\mathbb R$ such
that $\int_{\mathbb R^d}\Phi(|u(x)|/k)\,\mathrm{d} x<\infty$ for some $k>0$, normed by the
Luxemburg norm
$$
\|u\|_{L_\Phi}
:=\inf\Bigl\{k>0:\ \int_{\mathbb R^d}\Phi\Bigl(\frac{|u(x)|}{k}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x\le 1\Bigr\}.
$$
We write $L_\Phi^0$ for the closure of $C_0^\infty(\mathbb R^d)$ in $L_\Phi$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:MT}
A Young's function $\Phi$ is an \emph{$N$-function} if it is finite and
positive and satisfies
$$
\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\Phi(x)}{x}=\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x}{\Phi(x)}=0 .
$$
The set of $N$-functions is denoted by $\mathcal{N}$. The \emph{Morse--Transue space}
\cite{MorseTransue1950} is
$$
M_\Phi:=\Bigl\{u\ \text{measurable}:\
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\Phi\Bigl(\frac{|u(x)|}{k}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x<\infty\ \text{for every }k>0
\Bigr\},
$$
equipped with the norm of $L_\Phi$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:inverse}
For $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ the \emph{generalised inverse}
$\Phi^{-1}:[0,\infty]\to[0,\infty]$ is defined by
$$
\Phi^{-1}(y):=\inf\{x> 0:\ \Phi(x)>y\},\qquad y\ge 0,
$$
with $\inf\emptyset=\infty$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:concave}
If $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ then $\Phi^{-1}$ is concave on $[0,\infty)$ and $x/\Phi^{-1}(x)$
is increasing on $(0,\infty)$. In particular
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dilate}
\Phi^{-1}(x)\ \le\ \Phi^{-1}(c x)\ \le\ c\,\Phi^{-1}(x),
\qquad x\ge0,\ c\ge1 .
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Concavity and the monotonicity of $x/\Phi^{-1}(x)$ are
\cite[Lemma 4.1]{Laister}. In \eqref{eq:dilate} the left inequality is the
monotonicity of $\Phi^{-1}$. For the right one, write
$x=c^{-1}(c x)+(1-c^{-1})\cdot0$; concavity of $\Phi^{-1}$ and
$\Phi^{-1}(0)\ge0$ give
$\Phi^{-1}(x)\ge c^{-1}\Phi^{-1}(c x)+(1-c^{-1})\Phi^{-1}(0)
\ge c^{-1}\Phi^{-1}(c x)$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:orliczbasic}
Let $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item \emph{(Lattice and Fatou properties.)} If $|u|\le|v|$ a.e.\ then
$\|u\|_{L_\Phi}\le\|v\|_{L_\Phi}$, and $v\in M_\Phi$ implies $u\in M_\Phi$. If
$u_j\to u$ a.e.\ then $\|u\|_{L_\Phi}\le\liminf_{j}\|u_j\|_{L_\Phi}$.
\item There exist $c>0$ and $C_0\ge0$ such that $\Phi(z)\ge cz-C_0$ for all
$z\ge0$; in particular $\Phi(z)\to\infty$ as $z\to\infty$.
\item If $\Phi$ is finite, then
$\|h\|_{L_\Phi}\le\max\{1,\Phi(1)\}\bigl(\|h\|_{L^1}+\|h\|_{L^\infty}\bigr)$ for
every $h\in L^1\cap L^\infty$, and $L^1\cap L^\infty\subseteq M_\Phi$.
\item $L_\Phi$ is a Banach space and $M_\Phi$ and $L^0_\Phi$ are closed
subspaces of it. If $\Phi$ is finite then $L^0_\Phi\subseteq M_\Phi$; if
$\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ then $L^0_\Phi=M_\Phi$; if $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ satisfies the
$\Delta_2$-condition $\Phi(2x)\le C\Phi(x)$, $x\ge0$, then
$L^0_\Phi=M_\Phi=L_\Phi$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
(i) The first two statements are immediate from the monotonicity of $\Phi$.
If $u_j\to u$ a.e.\ and $k>\liminf_j\|u_j\|_{L_\Phi}$, then along a subsequence
$\int\Phi(|u_j|/k)\le1$, and since $\Phi$ is increasing and lower
semicontinuous, Fatou's lemma gives $\int\Phi(|u|/k)\le1$.

(ii) If $\Phi=0$ on $[0,x_\infty^\Phi)$ then $\Phi(z)\ge z-x_\infty^\Phi$.
Otherwise there is $z_0>0$ with $0<\Phi(z_0)<\infty$, and convexity together
with $\Phi(0)=0$ gives $\Phi(z)\ge (z/z_0)\Phi(z_0)$ for $z\ge z_0$; hence
$\Phi(z)\ge c(z-z_0)$ with $c=\Phi(z_0)/z_0$.

(iii) Let $k:=\max\{1,\Phi(1)\}(\|h\|_{L^1}+\|h\|_{L^\infty})>0$. Then
$|h|/k\le1$ a.e., so by convexity
$\Phi(|h|/k)\le(|h|/k)\Phi(1)$ and
$\int\Phi(|h|/k)\le\Phi(1)\|h\|_{L^1}/k\le1$. The same computation with $k$
replaced by any $k'>0$, applied to $h/k'$, shows $h\in M_\Phi$.

(iv) These facts are \cite[Remark 2.1(a), Lemma 4.2]{Laister}; the equality
$L^0_\Phi=M_\Phi$ for $N$-functions relies on
\cite[Theorem 8.21(d)]{Adams}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:minkowski}
Let $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$, let $I\subseteq\mathbb R$ be an interval and let
$g:\mathbb R^d\times I\to[0,\infty]$ be measurable. Then $s\mapsto\|g(\cdot,s)\|_{L_\Phi}$
is measurable and
$$
\Bigl\|\int_I g(\cdot,s)\,\mathrm{d} s\Bigr\|_{L_\Phi}\ \le\ \int_I\|g(\cdot,s)\|_{L_\Phi}\,\mathrm{d} s .
$$
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
For rational $k>0$ the function $s\mapsto\int\Phi(g(x,s)/k)\,\mathrm{d} x$ is measurable
by Tonelli's theorem, and
$\{s:\|g(\cdot,s)\|_{L_\Phi}<a\}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb Q\cap(0,a)}
\{s:\int\Phi(g(x,s)/k)\,\mathrm{d} x\le1\}$, which proves measurability. Let
$N(s):=\|g(\cdot,s)\|_{L_\Phi}$ and assume $A:=\int_IN(s)\,\mathrm{d} s<\infty$. Fix
$\varepsilon>0$ and a positive $e\in L^1(I)$ with $\int_Ie=\varepsilon$, and put
$k(s):=N(s)+e(s)>N(s)$ and $K:=\int_Ik=A+\varepsilon$. By the definition of
the Luxemburg norm, $\int\Phi(g(x,s)/k(s))\,\mathrm{d} x\le1$ for every $s$. For fixed
$x$, Jensen's inequality for the probability measure $k(s)\,\mathrm{d} s/K$ and the
lower semicontinuous convex function $\Phi$ gives
$$
\Phi\Bigl(\frac1K\int_Ig(x,s)\,\mathrm{d} s\Bigr)
=\Phi\Bigl(\int_I\frac{g(x,s)}{k(s)}\,\frac{k(s)\,\mathrm{d} s}{K}\Bigr)
\le\int_I\Phi\Bigl(\frac{g(x,s)}{k(s)}\Bigr)\frac{k(s)\,\mathrm{d} s}{K};
$$
if the mean on the left is infinite, the right-hand side is infinite as well
by Lemma~\ref{lem:orliczbasic}(ii). Integrating in $x$ and using Tonelli's
theorem, $\int\Phi\bigl(K^{-1}\int_Ig\,\mathrm{d} s\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x\le\int_Ik(s)\,\mathrm{d} s/K=1$,
whence $\|\int_Ig\,\mathrm{d} s\|_{L_\Phi}\le A+\varepsilon$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:complete}
Let $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$, $T\in(0,\infty]$, and let $w:Q_T\to[0,\infty)$ be measurable.
Let $X$ be the set of (equivalence classes of) measurable $u:Q_T\to\mathbb R$ with
$|u|\le w$ a.e.\ in $Q_T$, and put
$d(u,v):=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{t\in(0,T)}\|u(t)-v(t)\|_{L_\Phi}$. Then every sequence in $X$
{which is Cauchy with respect to $d$ converges, with respect to $d$, to an
element of $X$.} In particular, $(X,d)$ is a complete metric space whenever $d$ is finite on
$X\times X$, for instance if $\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_t\|w(t)\|_{L_\Phi}<\infty$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The measurability of $t\mapsto\|u(t)-v(t)\|_{L_\Phi}$ follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:minkowski}. Let $(u_j)$ be $d$-Cauchy and
$\varepsilon_j:=\sup_{k\ge j}d(u_j,u_k)\to0$. Discarding a null set of times,
$\|u_j(t)-u_k(t)\|_{L_\Phi}\le\varepsilon_j$ for all $k\ge j$ and all remaining
$t$. If $\|h\|_{L_\Phi}\le\varepsilon$ then $\int\Phi(|h|/\varepsilon')\le1$
for every $\varepsilon'>\varepsilon$, so that
$|\{x:|h(x)|>\delta\}|\le1/\Phi(\delta/\varepsilon')$ for every $\delta>0$.
Integrating in $t$, for every $R,T'\in(0,\infty)$ with $T'\le T$ and every
$\delta>0$,
$$
\bigl|\{(x,t)\in B_R\times(0,T'):|u_j-u_k|>\delta\}\bigr|\le
\frac{T'}{\Phi(\delta/(2\varepsilon_j))}\longrightarrow0\qquad(j\to\infty),
$$
by Lemma~\ref{lem:orliczbasic}(ii). Thus $(u_j)$ is Cauchy in measure on each
$B_R\times(0,T')$, and a diagonal argument yields a subsequence $(u_{j_i})$
and a measurable $u$ with $u_{j_i}\to u$ a.e.\ in $Q_T$; clearly $|u|\le w$.
By Fubini's theorem, for a.e.\ $t$ we have $u_{j_i}(t)\to u(t)$ a.e.\ in
$\mathbb R^d$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:orliczbasic}(i) gives
$\|u(t)-u_k(t)\|_{L_\Phi}\le\liminf_i\|u_{j_i}(t)-u_k(t)\|_{L_\Phi}\le
\varepsilon_k$. Hence $d(u,u_k)\le\varepsilon_k\to0$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:stable}
Let $0<\beta\le1$ and $t>0$, {and let $\Gamma$ denote Euler's Gamma
function.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For every $q>0$,
$\int_0^\infty s^{-q}\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s=t^{-q/\beta}\,
\Gamma(1+q/\beta)/\Gamma(1+q)$.
\item If $\beta<1$, then
$\int_0^\infty s^{r}\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s=t^{r/\beta}\,\Gamma(1-r/\beta)/\Gamma(1-r)$
for $0<r<\beta$, while the integral diverges for $r\ge\beta$. If $\beta=1$, it
equals $t^r$ for every $r>0$.
\item $\int_0^\infty\min\{1,s\}\,\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s\le(1-e^{-1})^{-1}(1-e^{-t})\le
2t$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
For $\beta=1$ all statements are immediate, so let $\beta<1$.

(i) Insert the identity
$s^{-q}=\Gamma(q)^{-1}\int_0^\infty\sigma^{q-1}e^{-\sigma s}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma$ and use Tonelli's theorem and \eqref{p1}:
$$
\int_0^\infty s^{-q}\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s=\frac{1}{\Gamma(q)}\int_0^\infty
\sigma^{q-1}e^{-t\sigma^\beta}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma
=\frac{t^{-q/\beta}\,\Gamma(q/\beta)}{\beta\,\Gamma(q)}
=t^{-q/\beta}\,\frac{\Gamma(1+q/\beta)}{\Gamma(1+q)} .
$$
(ii) For $0<r<1$ one has
$s^{r}=\frac{r}{\Gamma(1-r)}\int_0^\infty(1-e^{-\sigma s})\sigma^{-1-r}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma$,
so that, by Tonelli's theorem and \eqref{p1},
$$
\int_0^\infty s^r\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s=\frac{r}{\Gamma(1-r)}\int_0^\infty
\bigl(1-e^{-t\sigma^\beta}\bigr)\sigma^{-1-r}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma .
$$ Near $\sigma=0$ the integrand behaves like
$t\sigma^{\beta-1-r}$, which is integrable if and only if $r<\beta$; for $r<\beta$
the substitution $\tau=t\sigma^\beta$ gives the stated value. For $r\ge1$
the divergence follows from the case $r'\in[\beta,1)$ and the inequality
$s^r\ge s^{r'}-1$, $s>0$.
(iii) Since $1-e^{-s}\ge(1-e^{-1})\min\{1,s\}$ for $s\ge0$, the claim follows
from \eqref{p1} with $\sigma=1$ and $1-e^{-t}\le t$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:betarange}
The restriction $0<\beta\le1$ is used throughout and cannot be dispensed with
by the present methods. For $\beta>1$ the subordination formula \eqref{E7.4}
is unavailable, and the semigroup $e^{-t\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}^{\beta}}$ is in general not
positivity preserving; already the kernel of the free biharmonic semigroup
$e^{-t\Delta^2}$ changes sign. Fixed-time $L^p$--$L^q$ estimates for
$e^{-t\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}^\beta}$ with $\beta>1$ are available in a restricted range of
exponents \cite{BMMfrac}. Every argument below that uses the positivity of
the kernel would have to be rethought; this concerns the contraction and
comparison properties, the barrier construction in the fixed point argument,
and the whole of Section~\ref{sec:nonexistence}. We refer to
Section~\ref{sec:open} for further comments.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:solutions}
Let $T\in(0,\infty]$ and let $u_0$ be measurable.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item A measurable and almost everywhere finite $u:Q_T\to\mathbb R$ is an
\emph{integral solution} of \eqref{eq:main} in $Q_T$ if $S_\beta(t)|u_0|$ and
$\int_0^tS_\beta(t-s)|f(u(s))|\,\mathrm{d} s$ are finite a.e.\ in $Q_T$ and
$\mathcal F(u;u_0)=u$ a.e.\ in $Q_T$.
\item An integral solution $u$ is called an \emph{$M_\Phi$-mild solution} on
$[0,T)$ if, in addition, $u\in C\bigl([0,T),M_\Phi\bigr)$ and $u(0)=u_0$.
\item An $M_\Phi$-mild solution is a \emph{bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution} if
moreover $u\in L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}\bigl((0,T),L^\infty\bigr)$, that is,
$u\in L^\infty(\mathbb R^d\times(a,b))$ whenever $0<a<b<T$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:classical}
We work throughout with bounded mild solutions rather than with classical
ones: for $\beta<1$ the operator $\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}^{\beta}$ is nonlocal and the class
$C^{2,1}$ is no longer the natural one.

For $\beta=1$, every bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution $u$ on $[0,T)$ is a
classical solution in $Q_T$, as in \cite[Section 2.3]{Laister} for
$\varrho=0$. Since this fact is not used anywhere in the paper, we only sketch
the argument. Fix $0<a<b<T$ and let $M$ bound $|u|$ on $\mathbb R^d\times(a,b)$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}(iii), for $t\in(a,b)$ we have
$u(t)=e^{-(t-a)\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}}u(a)+v(t)$, where
$v(t):=\int_a^te^{-(t-s)\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}}g(s)\,\mathrm{d} s$ and $g:=f(u)$ satisfies
$|g|\le M\ell(M)$ on $\mathbb R^d\times(a,b)$. The Mehler kernel \eqref{mc} is smooth
for $t>0$, solves $\partial_t\mathcal{K}_t=-\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}\mathcal{K}_t$, and its derivatives are bounded,
locally uniformly in $(x,t)$, by polynomials times Gaussians in $y$. Hence
the first term is smooth in $\mathbb R^d\times(a,b)$ and solves the homogeneous
equation classically. The second term is a bounded distributional solution of
$\partial_tv-\Delta v+\varrho|x|^2v=g$ in $\mathbb R^d\times(a,b)$. Since the
coefficients are smooth and $g$ is bounded, interior $L^p$ estimates give
$v\in W^{2,1}_{p,\mathrm{loc}}$ for every $p<\infty$, and the parabolic Sobolev
embedding shows that $v$, and hence $u$, is locally H\"older continuous in
$(x,t)$ \cite{LSU,Lieberman}. As $f$ is locally Lipschitz, $g=f(u)$ is then
locally H\"older continuous as well, and the interior Schauder estimates
\cite{LSU,Lieberman} yield $v\in C^{2+\alpha,1+\alpha/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for
some $\alpha\in(0,1)$. We conclude that $u\in C^{2,1}(Q_T)$ and that $u$
satisfies $\partial_tu-\Delta u+\varrho|x|^2u=f(u)$ pointwise in $Q_T$.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:gronwall}
Let $0<\tau<\infty$, let $k\in L^1(0,\tau)$ be nonnegative, let $a\ge0$ and let
$y:(0,\tau]\to[0,\infty)$ be bounded and measurable with
$y(t)\le a+\int_0^tk(s)y(s)\,\mathrm{d} s$ for a.e.\ $t\in(0,\tau]$. Then
$y(t)\le a\exp\bigl(\int_0^tk(s)\,\mathrm{d} s\bigr)$ for a.e.\ $t\in(0,\tau]$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The function $Y(t):=a+\int_0^tky$ is absolutely continuous, and
$Y'=ky\le kY$ a.e. Hence $\bigl(Y(t)e^{-\int_0^tk}\bigr)'\le0$ a.e., so that
$Y(t)\le ae^{\int_0^tk}$, and $y\le Y$ a.e.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:OrliczSmoothing}
Let $0<\beta\le1$ and $\varrho>0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item \emph{(Semigroup properties.)}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For every $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$, every $t\ge0$ and every $\varphi\in L_\Phi$,
\begin{equation}\label{E5.2}
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\ \le\ m_\beta(t)\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi},
\end{equation}
and $S_\beta(t)M_\Phi\subseteq M_\Phi$.
\item If $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ is finite, then $\|S_\beta(t)\varphi-\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\to0$
as $t\to0$ for every $\varphi\in L^0_\Phi$.
\item If $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$, then $(S_\beta(t))_{t\ge0}$ is a $C_0$-semigroup of
contractions on $M_\Phi$.
\item The exponential rate in \eqref{E5.2} is sharp: for every $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$
and $t\ge0$ the operator norm of $S_\beta(t)$ on $L_\Phi$ is at least
$e^{-\lambda_\beta t}$.
\end{enumerate}
\item \emph{(Smoothing.)} Let $\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{Y}$ be compatible in the sense of
\eqref{E3.1}. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:OrliczOrlicz}
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L_\Psi}
\ \le\ \frac{2\kappa}{b}\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\,
\frac{\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)}
     {\Psi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)},
\qquad t>0,\ \varphi\in L_\Phi .
\end{equation}
\item \emph{(Small times.)} If in addition $\Phi$ is finite and
$\Psi^{-1}(s)/\Phi^{-1}(s)\to0$ as $s\to\infty$, then
$$
\lim_{t\to0}\ \frac{\Psi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)}
{\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)}\,\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L_\Psi}=0
$$
uniformly for $\varphi$ in compact subsets of $L^0_\Phi$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:Linfty}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$ and $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$. Then, with $C_\infty:=2\kappa$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:MTtoLinfty}
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}
\ \le\ C_\infty\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\,\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr),
\qquad t>0,\ \varphi\in L_\Phi .
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:Linftyzero}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$ and let $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ be finite. Then
$$
\lim_{t\to0}\
\frac{\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}}{\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)}=0
$$
uniformly for $\varphi$ in compact subsets of $L_\Phi^0$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:decay}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$, $\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{Y}$ compatible with constant $b$,
and let $\lambda_\beta$ be as in \eqref{eq:gap}. For every $\theta\in(0,1)$ and
every $\varphi\in L_\Phi$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:largetime}
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}
\ \le\ C_\infty\,2^{\frac d2}\theta^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\,
e^{-(1-\theta)\lambda_\beta t}\,
\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi},
\qquad t>0,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:largetime-sharp}
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}
\ \le\ C_\infty\,2^{\frac d2}e^{\lambda_\beta}\,\Phi^{-1}(1)\,
e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi},\qquad t\ge1,
\end{equation}
and the same estimates hold for $\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L_\Psi}$ with $C_\infty$
replaced by $2\kappa/b$ and $\Phi^{-1}$ replaced by $\Phi^{-1}/\Psi^{-1}$.
The rate $\lambda_\beta$ in \eqref{eq:largetime-sharp} cannot be improved.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:onLpLq}
Take $\Phi(x)=x^q/q$ and $\Psi(x)=x^r/r$ with $1\le q\le r<\infty$. Then
$\Phi^{-1}(x)=(qx)^{1/q}$ and $\Psi^{-1}(x)=(rx)^{1/r}$, and the choice
\eqref{eq:Theta-def} below gives
$\Theta^{-1}(x)=r^{1/r}q^{-1/q}x^{1+\frac1r-\frac1q}$. This function is concave
since $0<1+\frac1r-\frac1q\le1$. Thus \eqref{E3.1} holds with $b=1$ and
$\Theta(x)=C(q,r)\,x^{\frac{qr}{qr+q-r}}$, and \eqref{eq:OrliczOrlicz} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:LpLq}
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^r}\ \le\ C\,t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}
\left(\frac1q-\frac1r\right)}\|\varphi\|_{L^q},\qquad t>0 .
\end{equation}
Choose instead $\Psi=\Phi_2^\infty$, where $\Phi^\infty(x)=0$ for
$x\in[0,1]$, $\Phi^\infty(x)=\infty$ for $x>1$, and
$\Phi^\infty_2(x)=\Phi^\infty(x/2)$. Then $L_\Psi=L^\infty$, with
$\|\cdot\|_{L^\infty}=\frac12\|\cdot\|_{L_\Psi}$ and $\Psi^{-1}\equiv2$
\cite[Example 2.1(a)]{Laister}. With $\Theta=\Phi^*$, condition \eqref{E3.1}
holds with $b=1/2$ by \eqref{eq:24}, and \eqref{eq:OrliczOrlicz} reduces to
\eqref{eq:MTtoLinfty} with the constant $2\kappa$. We shall nevertheless give
a direct proof of Corollary \ref{cor:Linfty}.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:LpLq}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$ and $1\le q\le r\le\infty$. Then
\eqref{eq:LpLq} holds for all $t>0$ with a constant $C=C(d,\beta,q,r)$, and
for every $\theta\in(0,1)$ its right-hand side may be multiplied by
$C_\theta e^{-(1-\theta)\lambda_\beta t}$. Moreover
$\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^r}\le Ce^{-\lambda_\beta t}\|\varphi\|_{L^q}$ for
$t\ge1$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:thetachoice}
Suppose that the function
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Theta-def}
g(x):=\frac{x\,\Psi^{-1}(x)}{\Phi^{-1}(x)},\qquad x>0,\qquad g(0):=0,
\end{equation}
is a concave, increasing bijection of $[0,\infty)$. Then its inverse $\Theta$
is convex, increasing and finite, hence a Young's function with
$\Theta^{-1}=g$, and \eqref{E3.1} holds with $b=1$; compare
\cite[Remark 3.1(ii)]{Laister}.
Concavity is necessary, since the generalised inverse of every Young's
function is concave (Lemma~\ref{lem:concave}). Note that the right-hand
inequality in \eqref{E3.1} is the hypothesis
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compat}
\Theta^{-1}(x)\,\Phi^{-1}(x)\le x\,\Psi^{-1}(x),\qquad x\ge0 ,
\end{equation}
of O'Neil's convolution inequality (Lemma~\ref{lem:oneil}); the choice
$\Theta^{-1}=g$ turns it into an equality.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex:smoothing}
The following estimates follow from Corollaries~\ref{cor:Linfty} and
\ref{cor:decay} and Theorem~\ref{thm:OrliczSmoothing}(b); they are the
fractional Hermite counterparts of \cite[Examples 3.2--3.5]{Laister}. We put
$a:=d/(2\beta)$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $\Phi(x)=\max\{x-1,0\}$, so that $L_\Phi=L^1+L^\infty$ and
$\Phi^{-1}(x)=1+x$ \cite[Example 2.1(c)]{Laister}:
$\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\le C(1+t^{-a})\|\varphi\|_{L^1+L^\infty}$,
and $\le Ce^{-\lambda_\beta t}\|\varphi\|_{L^1+L^\infty}$ for $t\ge1$.
\item $\Phi(x)=\exp\exp(x^q)-e$, $q\ge1$, so that
$\Phi^{-1}(y)=\bigl(\log\log(e+y)\bigr)^{1/q}$. Then, for $t>0$,
$\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\le C\bigl(\log\log(e+t^{-a})\bigr)^{1/q}
\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}$.
\item $\Phi(x)=e^{x^q}-1$ and $\Psi(x)=e^{x^r}-1$ with $1\le q\le r$: by
\cite[Example 3.4]{Laister}, the function
$\Theta^{-1}(x)=x(\log(1+x))^{\frac1r-\frac1q}$ is concave, increasing and
invertible, and \eqref{eq:OrliczOrlicz} gives
$\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{\exp L^r}\le C\bigl(\log(1+t^{-a})\bigr)^{\frac1q-\frac1r}
\|\varphi\|_{\exp L^q}$.
\item $\Phi(x)=x^q/q$ and $\Psi(x)=e^{x^r}-1$ with $1\le q\le r$: by
\cite[Example 3.5]{Laister} the function
$\Theta^{-1}(x)=C(q)x^{1-\frac1q}(\log(1+x))^{\frac1r}$ is admissible, and
$\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{\exp L^r}\le Ct^{-\frac{a}{q}}
\bigl(\log(1+t^{-a})\bigr)^{-\frac1r}\|\varphi\|_{L^q}$.
\end{enumerate}
In (b)--(d), the right-hand sides may be multiplied by
$C_\theta e^{-(1-\theta)\lambda_\beta t}$. The Young's functions of (c) and (d)
do not depend on the operator, so the compatibility conditions are exactly
those verified in \cite{Laister}. The operator enters only through the
constant $2\kappa/b$ and the exponent $a$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:local}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$, $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ and let $f$ satisfy
$(\mathrm{L})$. Suppose that \eqref{eq:IC-infty} holds for some $\lambda>0$,
where $p_\ast=1+2\beta/d$. Then the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item \emph{(Uniform existence.)} For every compact subset $\mathcal K$ of
$M_\Phi$ there is $T_{\mathcal K}>0$ such that for every $u_0\in\mathcal K$
problem \eqref{eq:main} has a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution $u(\cdot;u_0)$ on
$[0,T_{\mathcal K})$ satisfying $|u(t;u_0)|\le 2S_\beta(t)|u_0|$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:smallrate}
\lim_{t\to0}\
\frac{\|u(t;u_0)\|_{L^\infty}}{\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)}=0
\end{equation}
uniformly for $u_0\in\mathcal K$. If $f$ is positone and $u_0\ge0$ (resp.
$u_0\le0$) then $u(t;u_0)\ge0$ (resp. $u(t;u_0)\le0$).
\item \emph{(Uniqueness.)} For every $T>0$, a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution of
\eqref{eq:main} on $[0,T)$ with data $u_0\in M_\Phi$ is unique. Consequently
$u(\cdot;u_0)$ extends to a maximal interval $[0,T_{u_0})$ as a bounded
$M_\Phi$-mild solution and, if $T_{u_0}<\infty$, then
$\|u(t)\|_{L^\infty}\to\infty$ as $t\uparrow T_{u_0}$.
\item \emph{(Continuous dependence.)} For every compact $\mathcal K\subseteq
M_\Phi$ there exist $C_{\mathcal K}>0$ and
$\tau_{\mathcal K}\in(0,T_{\mathcal K})$ such that for all
$\phi,\varphi\in\mathcal K$ and $t\in(0,\tau_{\mathcal K}]$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:contdep}
\|u(t;\phi)-u(t;\varphi)\|_{L_\Phi}
+\frac{\|u(t;\phi)-u(t;\varphi)\|_{L^\infty}}
      {\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)}
\ \le\ C_{\mathcal K}\,\|\phi-\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}.
\end{equation}
\item \emph{(Comparison principle.)} If $f$ is increasing and
$\phi\le\varphi$ in $M_\Phi$ then $u(t;\phi)\le u(t;\varphi)$ on
$[0,\min\{T_\phi,T_\varphi\})$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:global}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$, $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ and let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm L)$.
Suppose that \eqref{eq:IC-zero} holds for some $\lambda>0$, that is,
$$
I_0:=\frac{2\beta}{d}\int_0^{\infty}x^{-p_\ast}
\ell\bigl(\lambda\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x<1,
\qquad\text{and set}\qquad A:=(1-I_0)^{-1}\in[1,\infty).
$$
Then for every $u_0\in M_\Phi$ with $\|u_0\|_{L_\Phi}\le\lambda/(AC_\infty)$,
problem \eqref{eq:main} has a unique global bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution
$u$; it satisfies $|u(t)|\le A\,S_\beta(t)|u_0|$ for all $t>0$, and
\begin{align}
\|u(t)\|_{L^\infty}&\le\lambda\,\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)
\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}0,\qquad t>0,\label{eq:globaldecay1}\\
\|u(t)\|_{L_\Phi}&\le A\,m_\beta(t)\,\|u_0\|_{L_\Phi},\qquad t\ge0,
\label{eq:globaldecay2}\\
\|u(t)\|_{L^\infty}&\le C_\infty 2^{\frac d2}e^{\lambda_\beta}\Phi^{-1}(1)\,A\,
e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\,\|u_0\|_{L_\Phi},\qquad t\ge1 .\label{eq:expdecay}
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:confined}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$, $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$, and let $f$ satisfy
$(\mathrm L)$ and \eqref{eq:IC-infty} for some $\lambda>0$. Assume moreover
the Dini condition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dini}
\int_0^1\frac{\ell(r)}{r}\,\mathrm{d} r<\infty .
\end{equation}
Then there exists $\delta>0$ such that for every $u_0\in M_\Phi$ with
$\|u_0\|_{L_\Phi}\le\delta$ problem \eqref{eq:main} has a unique global bounded
$M_\Phi$-mild solution $u$. It satisfies $|u(t)|\le2S_\beta(t)|u_0|$ for all
$t>0$, and
\begin{gather*}
\|u(t)\|_{L_\Phi}\le2m_\beta(t)\|u_0\|_{L_\Phi}\quad(t\ge0),\\
\|u(t)\|_{L^\infty}\le 2C_\infty2^{\frac d2}e^{\lambda_\beta}\Phi^{-1}(1)\,
e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\|u_0\|_{L_\Phi}\quad(t\ge1).
\end{gather*}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\rm[No Fujita phenomenon under confinement]\label{rem:nofujita}
Let $f(u)=|u|^{m-1}u$ with $m>1$, so that $\ell(s)=ms^{m-1}$ and
\eqref{eq:dini} holds, and let $\Phi(x)=x^q/q$ with $q>\max\{1,q_c\}$, where
$q_c:=d(m-1)/(2\beta)$; then \eqref{eq:IC-infty} holds
(Section~\ref{subsec:power}). Theorem~\ref{thm:confined} thus gives global
solutions with exponential decay for small data in $L^q$, for \emph{every}
$m>1$. For the free equation, this fails when $1<m\le p_\ast$, since every
nonnegative nontrivial solution then blows up in finite time
\cite{Fujita1966,Hayakawa1973,KST1977,Sugitani1975}. The spectral gap of
$\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}^\beta$ thus removes the Fujita phenomenon, as a bounded domain with
Dirichlet boundary conditions does. Moreover, the condition near $x=0$ in
\eqref{eq:IC-zero}, which encodes the free decay rate
$\Phi^{-1}(t^{-d/(2\beta)})$, becomes superfluous. This is why
Corollary~\ref{thm:global} is a genuinely ``free'' statement, while
Theorem~\ref{thm:confined} is specific to $\varrho>0$.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\rm[Why $M_\Phi$?]\label{rem:whyMT}
For a general Young's function $\Phi$, the space $C_0^\infty$ need not be
dense in $L_\Phi$, and $S_\beta(t)$ need not be strongly continuous on
$L_\Phi$ at $t=0$. The standard example is $\Phi(u)=e^{u^2}-1$, see
\cite[Section 2]{ioku2015MPAG} and \cite[Section 1.1]{Laister}. The
Morse--Transue subspace restores the $C_0$-semigroup property, and for
$N$-functions it coincides with $L^0_\Phi$. If $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ satisfies in
addition the $\Delta_2$-condition, then $M_\Phi=L_\Phi$ and all the results
above hold in $L_\Phi$ itself.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\rm[On the constants]\label{rem:constants}
The constant $2\beta/d$ in Corollary~\ref{thm:global} comes from
\eqref{eq:changevar}; it reduces to $2/n$ when $\beta=1$, $d=n$, in agreement
with \cite[(3.9)]{Laister}. The value $A=(1-I_0)^{-1}$ is the smallest number
for which the barrier $w=AS_\beta(t)|u_0|$ is invariant under the Duhamel
map, as the proof in Section~\ref{sec:proofB} shows.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:sharper}
Since $S_\beta(t)$ is a contraction on $L_\Phi$, rather than merely bounded
by $2$ as in \cite[Theorem A(a)(i)]{Laister}, the contraction constant of the
Duhamel map in the global argument is $I_0$, and not $2I_0$. This is why the
threshold in \eqref{eq:IC-zero} is $d/(2\beta)$, whereas the argument of
\cite[Section 5.5]{Laister} leads to $n/4$ for $\beta=1$. Since the Gaussian
semigroup is likewise a contraction on every Orlicz space
(Lemma~\ref{L5.1}), the same observation improves the threshold $n/4$ of
\cite[Corollary B]{Laister} to $n/2$ also for $\varrho=0$.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:onG}
Hypothesis $(\mathrm G)$ encodes the class of nonlinearities of the form
\begin{equation}\label{E7.1}
f(u)=e^{u^{\rho}\mu(u)} ,
\end{equation}
with $\mu$ slowly varying, in which case $\log f$ is regularly varying of index
$\rho$; the value $\rho=\infty$ is admitted and corresponds to $\log f$ rapidly
varying, that is, to a nonlinearity of unrestricted growth rate
\cite[Lemma 6.1(i)]{Laister}. By \cite[Lemma 6.1(iv)]{Laister} the limit in
$(\mathrm G)$ is necessarily equal to $1$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limitone}
\lim_{u\to\infty}f'(u)F(u)=1 .
\end{equation}
Under $(\mathrm C)$ one has $f(s)\le sf(1)$ for $s\in[0,1]$, so that
$\int_0^1\,\mathrm{d} s/f(s)=\infty$; hence $F$ is a decreasing $C^1$-bijection of
$(0,\infty)$ onto itself. Since $F'=-1/f$, its inverse satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{E7.3}
\bigl(F^{-1}\bigr)'(\sigma)=-f\bigl(F^{-1}(\sigma)\bigr),
\qquad \sigma>0 ,
\end{equation}
and \eqref{eq:limitone} may be rewritten as
\begin{equation}\label{E7.2}
\lim_{\sigma\to0^+}\sigma\,f'\bigl(F^{-1}(\sigma)\bigr)=1 .
\end{equation}
Identity \eqref{E7.2} is the form of $(\mathrm G)$ that we shall use in
Section~\ref{sec:nonexistence}. It is more precise than the two-sided bounds
$C^{-1}\sigma^{\varepsilon-1}\le f(F^{-1}(\sigma))\le C\sigma^{-\varepsilon-1}$
of \cite[Lemma 3.1]{Fujishima2024} (with $q_f=1$ in the notation there).
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:critical}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$, and let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$ and
$(\mathrm G)$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item \emph{(Local well-posedness.)}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item If $f$ satisfies $(\Lambda)$, $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ and $\Phi\gtrsim f$ at
infinity, then \eqref{eq:IC-infty} holds for some $\lambda>0$, and hence the
conclusions of Theorem~\ref{thm:local} hold.
\item If, in addition to the hypotheses of \textup{(i)}, there is $C>0$ with
$|f(u)|\le Cf(|u|)$ for all $u\in\mathbb R$, and $\Phi\gtrsim f^r$ near $0$ for some
$r\ge1$ with $r>d/(2\beta)$, then every $M_\Phi$-mild solution of
\eqref{eq:main} on $[0,T)$ with data $u_0\in M_\Phi$ is a bounded
$M_\Phi$-mild solution; in particular it coincides with $u(\cdot;u_0)$, and
$T\le T_{u_0}$.
\end{enumerate}
\item \emph{(Nonexistence.)} If $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ and $\Phi\lesssim f$ at infinity,
then there exists a nonnegative $u_0\in L_\Phi$ with compact support such that
\eqref{eq:main} has no nonnegative integral solution in $Q_T$ for any $T>0$.
\item \emph{(Global well-posedness.)} Suppose $f$ satisfies $(\Lambda_0)$ and
$f'(0)=0$, that $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ and $\Phi\gtrsim f$ at infinity, and that there
exists $\lambda_0>0$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:C-b}
\int_0^{1}x^{-p_\ast}f'\bigl(\lambda_0\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x<\infty .
\end{equation}
Then the conclusions of Theorem \ref{thm:local} and Corollary
\ref{thm:global} hold for some $\lambda\in(0,\lambda_0]$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:C}
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$, $(\Lambda_0)$ and $(\mathrm G)$, and suppose
that $f'$ is regularly varying at zero of index $m-1$ for some $m>p_\ast$. If
$\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ satisfies $\Phi(u)\gtrsim u^{q}$ near $0$ for some
$0<q<\frac{d(m-1)}{2\beta}$ and $\Phi\gtrsim f$ at infinity, then the
conclusions of Theorem \ref{thm:local} and Corollary \ref{thm:global} hold for
some $\lambda>0$.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor:Cconf}
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$, $(\Lambda_0)$ and $(\mathrm G)$, and assume that
$\int_0^1f'(r)\,\,\mathrm{d} r/r<\infty$ (for instance, $f'$ regularly varying at zero
of some index $m-1>0$). If $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ and $\Phi\gtrsim f$ at infinity, then
the conclusions of Theorem~\ref{thm:local} and Theorem~\ref{thm:confined}
hold.
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:kernel}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$ and $\omega=\sqrt\varrho$. Then for all
$t>0$ and $x,y\in\mathbb R^d$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)=\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(y,x)>0$, and for fixed $y$ the map
$(x,t)\mapsto\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)$ is continuous on $\mathbb R^d\times(0,\infty)$;
\item $\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)\le p_\beta(x-y,t)$, and consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:supkernel}
\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}_t^\beta(x,y)\ \le\ \kappa_{d,\beta}\,t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}},
\qquad \kappa_{d,\beta}=p_\beta(0,1)
=(4\pi)^{-\frac d2}\frac{\Gamma(1+\frac{d}{2\beta})}{\Gamma(1+\frac d2)};
\end{equation}
\item one has the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mass1}
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}_t(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} x
=\bigl(\cosh(2\omega t)\bigr)^{-d/2}
\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\omega\tanh(2\omega t)}{2}|y|^2\Bigr),
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:massbeta}
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} x=\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(y,x)\,\mathrm{d} x
\ \le\ m_\beta(t)=\min\bigl\{1,\ 2^{d/2}e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\bigr\};
\end{equation}
\item $S_\beta(t)h_0=e^{-\lambda_\beta t}h_0$ pointwise, where
$h_0(x)=e^{-\omega|x|^2/2}$;
\item if $\varphi\in L^1$, then $(x,t)\mapsto[S_\beta(t)\varphi](x)$ is
continuous on $\mathbb R^d\times(0,\infty)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
(i) Positivity, symmetry and continuity of $\mathcal{K}_t$ are clear from \eqref{mc}.
For $\beta<1$, positivity and symmetry of $\mathcal{K}^\beta_t$ follow from
\eqref{E7.5} and $\eta_t^\beta\ge0$. By \eqref{eq:scaling-eta},
$\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)=\int_0^\infty\mathcal{K}_{t^{1/\beta}\sigma}(x,y)\eta^\beta_1(\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma$;
the integrand is continuous in $(x,t)$ and, by (ii) below for $\beta=1$,
bounded by $(4\pi t^{1/\beta}\sigma)^{-d/2}$, which is integrable against
$\eta^\beta_1$ uniformly for $t$ in compact subsets of $(0,\infty)$, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:stable}(i). The continuity then follows from dominated
convergence.

(ii) We first treat $\beta=1$. Comparing the prefactors in \eqref{mc} and
\eqref{eq:freekernel}, we have $\frac{\omega}{2\pi\sinh(2\omega t)}\le
\frac{1}{4\pi t}$ because $\sinh z\ge z$ for $z\ge0$; and, discarding the
nonnegative term $|x+y|^2\tanh(\omega t)$ in the exponent and using $z\coth
z\ge1$ for $z>0$ with $z=\omega t$, we obtain $\frac{\omega}{4}\coth(\omega
t)\ge\frac{1}{4t}$. Hence $\mathcal{K}_t(x,y)\le G(x-y,t)$ for every $t>0$. (This is
also a consequence of the Feynman--Kac formula, since the potential
$\varrho|x|^2$ is nonnegative \cite{Simon1982}.) Integrating against
$\eta^\beta_t$ and recalling \eqref{E7.5} and \eqref{eq:freekernel}, we obtain
$\mathcal{K}_t^\beta(x,y)\le p_\beta(x-y,t)$. By \eqref{eq:scaling-eta} and the
self-similarity of the Gaussian,
$p_\beta(z,t)=t^{-d/(2\beta)}p_\beta\bigl(t^{-1/(2\beta)}z,1\bigr)$, and since
$G(\cdot,s)$ is radially decreasing, $\sup_z p_\beta(z,t)=p_\beta(0,t)=
t^{-d/(2\beta)}p_\beta(0,1)$. Finally
$p_\beta(0,1)=(4\pi)^{-d/2}\int_0^\infty s^{-d/2}\eta^\beta_1(s)\,\mathrm{d} s$, whose value
is given by Lemma~\ref{lem:stable}(i) with $q=d/2$.

(iii) Consider first $d=1$ and $\omega=1$, and write $a:=\tanh t$,
$c:=\coth t$ and $s:=\sinh 2t$. Since $a+c=2\cosh 2t/s$ and $c-a=2/s$, the
exponent in \eqref{mc} equals
$-\frac a4\bigl[(x+y)^2+(x-y)^2\bigr]-\frac{c-a}{4}(x-y)^2
=-\frac a2(x^2+y^2)-\frac{(x-y)^2}{2s}$, so that
$$
\mathcal{K}_t(x,y)=(2\pi s)^{-1/2}
\exp\Bigl(-\frac{a}{2}\bigl(x^2+y^2\bigr)-\frac{(x-y)^2}{2s}\Bigr).
$$
Integrating in $x$, the exponent is
$-\mathsf A x^2+\mathsf Bx-\tfrac{a}{2}y^2-\tfrac{y^2}{2s}$ with
$\mathsf A=\tfrac a2+\tfrac1{2s}=\tfrac{\cosh 2t}{2s}$ (because
$\tanh t=(\cosh 2t-1)/\sinh 2t$) and $\mathsf B=y/s$, whence
\begin{align*}
\int_\mathbb R\mathcal{K}_t(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} x
&=(2\pi s)^{-1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\mathsf A}}\,
\exp\Bigl(\frac{\mathsf B^2}{4\mathsf A}-\frac a2 y^2-\frac{y^2}{2s}\Bigr)\\
&=(\cosh 2t)^{-1/2}\exp\Bigl(-y^2\Bigl[\frac{\cosh 2t}{2s}
-\frac{1}{2s\cosh 2t}\Bigr]\Bigr).
\end{align*}
The bracket equals $\frac{\cosh^2 2t-1}{2s\cosh 2t}=\frac{\tanh 2t}{2}$, which
is \eqref{eq:mass1} for $d=1$, $\omega=1$. The kernel \eqref{mc} is a product
of one-dimensional kernels, which gives the case $d\ge1$, $\omega=1$. For
general $\omega$, the change of variables $x=\omega^{-1/2}z$ transforms
$\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}$ into $\omega\mathcal H_1$, so that
$\mathcal{K}_t(x,y)=\omega^{d/2}\mathcal{K}^{(1)}_{\omega t}(\omega^{1/2}x,\omega^{1/2}y)$, where
$\mathcal{K}^{(1)}$ is the kernel for $\omega=1$ (this can also be read off
directly from \eqref{mc}); integrating in $x$ gives \eqref{eq:mass1}.

Since $\cosh z\ge\max\{1,e^{z}/2\}$, we infer from \eqref{eq:mass1} that
$\int\mathcal{K}_t(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} x\le\min\{1,2^{d/2}e^{-d\omega t}\}$. Integrating against
$\eta_t^\beta$ and using \eqref{p1} with $\sigma=d\omega$, we get
$$
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} x
\le\min\Bigl\{1,\ 2^{d/2}\int_0^\infty e^{-d\omega s}\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s\Bigr\}
=\min\bigl\{1,\ 2^{d/2}e^{-t(d\omega)^{\beta}}\bigr\},
$$
which is \eqref{eq:massbeta}; the equality of the two integrals in
\eqref{eq:massbeta} is the symmetry from (i).

(iv) A direct computation gives $\mathcal{H}_{\varrho} h_0=d\omega h_0$, so that
$e^{-s\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}}h_0=e^{-sd\omega}h_0$ in $L^2$. Both sides are continuous functions
(the left-hand side by (v) with $\beta=1$), and hence the identity holds
pointwise. Integrating against $\eta^\beta_t$ and using \eqref{p1}, we obtain
the claim.

(v) By (i) and (ii), for $t\ge t_0>0$ the integrand
$\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)\varphi(y)$ is continuous in $(x,t)$ and bounded by
$\kappa_{d,\beta}t_0^{-d/(2\beta)}|\varphi(y)|$, and the conclusion follows
from dominated convergence.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:sharpgap}
The rate $\lambda_\beta=(d\sqrt\varrho\,)^\beta$ in \eqref{eq:massbeta} is
sharp, since it is the ground state energy of $\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}^\beta$; see \eqref{eq:gap}
and Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}(iv). This is the only point at which the confining
potential is used quantitatively; every other estimate in this section is
inherited from the free kernel $p_\beta$ through Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}(ii).
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:orlicznorm}
Let $\Theta\in\mathcal{Y}$ and let $k:\mathbb R^d\to[0,\infty)$ be measurable with
$\|k\|_{L^1}\le1$ and $\|k\|_{L^\infty}\le M$ for some $M\in(0,\infty)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kernel-Theta-raw}
\|k\|_{L_\Theta}\ \le\ \frac{M}{\Theta^{-1}(M)} .
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The value $\Theta^{-1}(M)$ is finite and strictly positive, so that
$\mu:=M/\Theta^{-1}(M)\in(0,\infty)$, and $\Theta(\Theta^{-1}(M))\le M<\infty$
by \eqref{eq:22}; in particular $\Theta$ is finite and convex on
$[0,\Theta^{-1}(M)]$. For almost every $x$ we have $k(x)/M\in[0,1]$ and
$$
\Theta\Bigl(\frac{k(x)}{\mu}\Bigr)
=\Theta\Bigl(\frac{k(x)}{M}\,\Theta^{-1}(M)\Bigr)
\le\frac{k(x)}{M}\,\Theta\bigl(\Theta^{-1}(M)\bigr)\le k(x),
$$
where the first inequality uses the convexity of $\Theta$ on
$[0,\Theta^{-1}(M)]$ together with $\Theta(0)=0$. Integrating over $\mathbb R^d$,
we get $\int_{\mathbb R^d}\Theta(k/\mu)\,\mathrm{d} x\le\|k\|_{L^1}\le1$, and
\eqref{eq:kernel-Theta-raw} follows from the definition of the Luxemburg norm.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- corollary-7: body_md -->
\label{cor:kernelTheta}
Let $0<\beta\le1$ and $\Theta\in\mathcal{Y}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kernel-Theta}
\bigl\|p_\beta(\cdot,t)\bigr\|_{L_\Theta}
\ \le\ \kappa\,\frac{t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}}
{\Theta^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)},
\qquad t>0 ,
\end{equation}
with $\kappa=\max\{1,\kappa_{d,\beta}\}$, and the same bound holds for
$\sup_{y}\|\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(\cdot,y)\|_{L_\Theta}$ and
$\sup_{x}\|\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,\cdot)\|_{L_\Theta}$. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:row-Lr}
\sup_{y\in\mathbb R^d}\bigl\|\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(\cdot,y)\bigr\|_{L^{r}}
\ \le\ \kappa_{d,\beta}^{1-\frac1r}\,t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}\left(1-\frac1r\right)},
\qquad t>0,\ r\in[1,\infty].
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-7 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Put $M_t:=\kappa\,t^{-d/(2\beta)}$. Then $\|p_\beta(\cdot,t)\|_{L^1}=1$ and,
by Lemma \ref{lem:kernel}(ii), $\|p_\beta(\cdot,t)\|_{L^\infty}\le M_t$. From
Lemma \ref{lem:orlicznorm} and the monotonicity of $\Theta^{-1}$, we deduce
$$
\bigl\|p_\beta(\cdot,t)\bigr\|_{L_\Theta}\le\frac{M_t}{\Theta^{-1}(M_t)}
\le\kappa\,\frac{t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}}
{\Theta^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)} ,
$$
where we used $\kappa\ge1$. The statement for $\mathcal{K}^\beta_t$ follows from
$0\le\mathcal{K}_t^\beta(\cdot,y)\le p_\beta(\cdot-y,t)$, the lattice property of
the Luxemburg norm and its translation invariance, together with the
symmetry of $\mathcal{K}^\beta_t$. Estimate \eqref{eq:row-Lr} is H\"older's
interpolation between $\|\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(\cdot,y)\|_{L^1}\le1$ and
$\|\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(\cdot,y)\|_{L^\infty}\le\kappa_{d,\beta}t^{-d/(2\beta)}$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:reproves}
Taking $\beta=1$ in Corollary \ref{cor:kernelTheta} gives
$$
\|G(\cdot,t)\|_{L_\Theta}\le \max\{1,(4\pi)^{-d/2}\}\,
\frac{t^{-d/2}}{\Theta^{-1}(t^{-d/2})},\qquad t>0,
$$
that is, \cite[Proposition 4.1]{Laister}, with a constant independent of
$\Theta$. The proof in \cite{Laister} proceeds through radial coordinates and
an induction on the dimension, carried out separately for even and odd
dimensions; its constant depends on $\Theta$ through $x_\infty^\Theta$.
Lemma \ref{lem:orlicznorm} shows that only the $L^1$ and $L^\infty$ norms of
the kernel enter. We do not know whether O'Neil's inequality itself extends to
general non-convolution kernels with uniform row and column bounds in
$L_\Theta$; the pointwise domination of Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}(ii) makes the
question irrelevant here.
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:strongcont}
Let $0<\beta\le1$ and $\varrho>0$. For every $\varphi\in C_0^\infty(\mathbb R^d)$,
$$
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi-\varphi\|_{L^1}\to0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi-\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\to0
\qquad\text{as }t\to0 .
$$
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Consider first $\beta=1$. Write $E_t(x,y):=G(x-y,t)-\mathcal{K}_t(x,y)\ge0$ (Lemma
\ref{lem:kernel}(ii)) and recall that
$\|G(\cdot,t)*\varphi-\varphi\|_{L^1}\to0$ and
$\|G(\cdot,t)*\varphi-\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\to0$ as $t\to0$. Put
$m_t(y):=\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}_t(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} x=\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}_t(y,x)\,\mathrm{d} x$; by
\eqref{eq:mass1}, $m_t(y)\uparrow1$ as $t\downarrow0$, uniformly for $y$ in
compact sets. Since $\int_{\mathbb R^d}G(x-y,t)\,\mathrm{d} x=1$, Tonelli's theorem yields
$$
\bigl\|G(\cdot,t)*\varphi-e^{-t\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}}\varphi\bigr\|_{L^1}
\le\int_{\mathbb R^d}\int_{\mathbb R^d}E_t(x,y)|\varphi(y)|\,\mathrm{d} x\,\mathrm{d} y
=\int_{\mathbb R^d}\bigl(1-m_t(y)\bigr)|\varphi(y)|\,\mathrm{d} y\longrightarrow0
$$
by dominated convergence, which proves the $L^1$ statement. For the
$L^\infty$ statement, let $R>0$ with $\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subseteq B_R$. For $|x|\le2R$,
\begin{align*}
\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb R^d}E_t(x,y)\varphi(y)\,\mathrm{d} y\Bigr|
&\le\|\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\int_{\mathbb R^d}E_t(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} y
=\|\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\bigl(1-m_t(x)\bigr)\\
&\le\|\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\sup_{|z|\le 2R}\bigl(1-m_t(z)\bigr)\longrightarrow0 .
\end{align*}
For $|x|>2R$ we have $\varphi(x)=0$, and since $|x-y|>R$ for $y\in B_R$,
$|e^{-t\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}}\varphi(x)|\le\|\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\int_{|z|>R}G(z,t)\,\mathrm{d} z\to0$
uniformly in $x$. Combining the two regimes with the uniform convergence of
$G(\cdot,t)*\varphi$ to $\varphi$ completes the proof for $\beta=1$.

For $0<\beta<1$, let $X$ be either $L^1$ or $L^\infty$. We use
\eqref{E7.4}, the identity $\int_0^\infty\eta_t^\beta=1$ (together with
Tonelli's theorem for $X=L^1$ and a pointwise estimate for $X=L^\infty$), and
the contractivity of $e^{-s\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}}$ on $X$. For every $\delta>0$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi-\varphi\|_{X}
&\le\int_0^{\delta}\bigl\|e^{-s\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}}\varphi-\varphi\bigr\|_{X}
\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s
+2\|\varphi\|_{X}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s\\
&\le\sup_{0<s\le\delta}\bigl\|e^{-s\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}}\varphi-\varphi\bigr\|_X
+2\|\varphi\|_X\int_{\delta t^{-1/\beta}}^{\infty}\eta_1^\beta(\sigma)
\,\mathrm{d}\sigma ,
\end{align*}
where the last step uses \eqref{eq:scaling-eta}. Given $\varepsilon>0$, choose
$\delta$ so that the first term is at most $\varepsilon/2$ by the case
$\beta=1$, and then $t$ so small that the second is at most $\varepsilon/2$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{L5.1}
Let $k:\mathbb R^d\times\mathbb R^d\to[0,\infty)$ be measurable, let $m>0$, and assume
$$
\sup_{x\in\mathbb R^d}\int_{\mathbb R^d}k(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} y\le m
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\sup_{y\in\mathbb R^d}\int_{\mathbb R^d}k(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} x\le m .
$$
Let $T\varphi(x):=\int_{\mathbb R^d}k(x,y)\varphi(y)\,\mathrm{d} y$. If $m\le1$, then for every
$\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$ and every $\varphi\in L_\Phi$ the integral defining $T\varphi$
converges absolutely a.e., and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Orlicz-op}
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\Phi\Bigl(\frac{|T\varphi(x)|}{\mu}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x
\ \le\ \int_{\mathbb R^d}\Phi\Bigl(\frac{|\varphi(y)|}{\mu}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d} y
\qquad\text{for every }\mu>0 .
\end{equation}
In particular $T(M_\Phi)\subseteq M_\Phi$. For every $m>0$ and $\Phi\in\mathcal{Y}$,
$\|T\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\le m\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Let $m\le1$, fix $\mu>0$ and set $M(x):=\int_{\mathbb R^d}k(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} y\in[0,1]$. If
$M(x)=0$ then $T\varphi(x)=0$, so assume $M(x)>0$. Since $\Phi$ is convex with
$\Phi(0)=0$ and $M(x)\le1$, we have $\Phi(M(x)z)\le M(x)\Phi(z)$ for $z\ge0$;
applying this with $z=M(x)^{-1}\int k(x,y)|\varphi(y)|\mu^{-1}\,\mathrm{d} y$, and then
Jensen's inequality for the probability measure $k(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} y/M(x)$ and the
lower semicontinuous convex function $\Phi$, yields
\begin{align*}
\Phi\Bigl(\frac{1}{\mu}\int_{\mathbb R^d}k(x,y)|\varphi(y)|\,\mathrm{d} y\Bigr)
&\le M(x)\,\Phi\Bigl(\frac{1}{M(x)}\int_{\mathbb R^d}k(x,y)\frac{|\varphi(y)|}{\mu}\,\mathrm{d} y\Bigr)\\
&\le\int_{\mathbb R^d}k(x,y)\,\Phi\Bigl(\frac{|\varphi(y)|}{\mu}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d} y .
\end{align*}
Integrating in $x$ and invoking Tonelli's theorem and the second hypothesis
on $k$, we obtain \eqref{eq:Orlicz-op} with $|T\varphi|$ replaced by
$\int k|\varphi|\,\mathrm{d} y\ge|T\varphi|$. If we choose $\mu$ with
$\int\Phi(|\varphi|/\mu)<\infty$, the right-hand side is finite, so
$\Phi(\mu^{-1}\int k(x,y)|\varphi(y)|\,\mathrm{d} y)<\infty$ for a.e.\ $x$, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:orliczbasic}(ii) shows that $\int k(x,y)|\varphi(y)|\,\mathrm{d} y<\infty$
for a.e.\ $x$. Taking $\mu>\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}$ makes the right-hand side of
\eqref{eq:Orlicz-op} at most $1$, whence $\|T\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\le\mu$. Letting
$\mu\downarrow\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}$, we get $\|T\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\le
\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}$. The invariance of $M_\Phi$ is immediate from
\eqref{eq:Orlicz-op}. For general $m>0$, it suffices to apply the case $m=1$
to the kernel $k/m$ and to use the homogeneity of the norm.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:oneil}
Let $\Phi,\Psi,\Theta\in\mathcal{Y}$ satisfy \eqref{eq:compat}. If $\varphi\in L_\Phi$
and $g\in L_\Theta$ then $g*\varphi\in L_\Psi$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Oneil}
\|g*\varphi\|_{L_\Psi}\ \le\ 2\,\|g\|_{L_\Theta}\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi} .
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
(a)(i) By Lemma \ref{lem:kernel}(iii), the kernel $\mathcal{K}^\beta_t$ satisfies the
hypotheses of Lemma \ref{L5.1} with $m=m_\beta(t)\le1$. We thus obtain
\eqref{E5.2} and the invariance of $M_\Phi$.

(a)(ii) Let $\Phi$ be finite, $\varphi\in L^0_\Phi$ and $\varepsilon>0$, and
choose $\psi\in C_0^\infty(\mathbb R^d)$ with $\|\varphi-\psi\|_{L_\Phi}<\varepsilon$. By
\eqref{E5.2} and Lemma~\ref{lem:orliczbasic}(iii), with
$c_\Phi:=\max\{1,\Phi(1)\}$,
\begin{align*}
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi-\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}
&\le\|S_\beta(t)(\varphi-\psi)\|_{L_\Phi}+\|S_\beta(t)\psi-\psi\|_{L_\Phi}
+\|\psi-\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\\
&\le 2\varepsilon+c_\Phi\bigl(\|S_\beta(t)\psi-\psi\|_{L^1}
+\|S_\beta(t)\psi-\psi\|_{L^\infty}\bigr).
\end{align*}
By Lemma \ref{lem:strongcont}, the last bracket tends to $0$ as $t\to0$.
Since $\varepsilon>0$ was arbitrary, the claim follows.

(a)(iii) If $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$, then $M_\Phi=L^0_\Phi$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:orliczbasic}(iv). By (i), $S_\beta(t)$ is a contraction on
$M_\Phi$. The semigroup property holds by Section~\ref{subsec:operator}, and
strong continuity at $t=0$ is (ii). Strong continuity at $t_0>0$ then follows
from the contraction property: for $0<h\le t_0$,
$\|S_\beta(t_0+h)\varphi-S_\beta(t_0)\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}
+\|S_\beta(t_0-h)\varphi-S_\beta(t_0)\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\le
2\|S_\beta(h)\varphi-\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}$.

(a)(iv) The Gaussian $h_0$ belongs to $L_\Phi$. Indeed, if $z_0>0$ satisfies
$\Phi(z_0)<\infty$ and $k\ge\|h_0\|_{L^\infty}/z_0=1/z_0$, then
$\Phi(h_0/k)\le(h_0/(kz_0))\Phi(z_0)$, which is integrable. Moreover
$\|h_0\|_{L_\Phi}>0$. By Lemma \ref{lem:kernel}(iv),
$\|S_\beta(t)h_0\|_{L_\Phi}=e^{-\lambda_\beta t}\|h_0\|_{L_\Phi}$.

(b) Let $\Theta\in\mathcal{Y}$ and $b\in(0,1]$ be as in \eqref{E3.1}. Since
$\mathcal{K}^\beta_t\ge0$, Lemma \ref{lem:kernel}(ii) gives the pointwise domination
$$
\bigl|S_\beta(t)\varphi(x)\bigr|
\le\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)|\varphi(y)|\,\mathrm{d} y
\le\bigl(p_\beta(\cdot,t)*|\varphi|\bigr)(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb R^d .
$$
The right-hand inequality in \eqref{E3.1} is \eqref{eq:compat}. Hence the
lattice property of the Luxemburg norm and Lemma \ref{lem:oneil} give
$$
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L_\Psi}
\le\bigl\|p_\beta(\cdot,t)*|\varphi|\bigr\|_{L_\Psi}
\le 2\,\bigl\|p_\beta(\cdot,t)\bigr\|_{L_\Theta}\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi} .
$$
Corollary \ref{cor:kernelTheta} bounds the middle factor by
$\kappa t^{-d/(2\beta)}/\Theta^{-1}(t^{-d/(2\beta)})$, while the left-hand
inequality in \eqref{E3.1} gives, for every $x>0$,
$$
\frac{x}{\Theta^{-1}(x)}\ \le\ \frac{1}{b}\,
\frac{\Phi^{-1}(x)}{\Psi^{-1}(x)} .
$$
Combining the two displays with $x=t^{-d/(2\beta)}$, we arrive at
\eqref{eq:OrliczOrlicz}.

(c) Put $\gamma_\ast(t):=\Psi^{-1}(t^{-d/(2\beta)})/\Phi^{-1}(t^{-d/(2\beta)})$,
so that $\gamma_\ast(t)\to0$ as $t\to0$, and let $\mathcal K\subseteq L^0_\Phi$
be compact. Suppose that there are $\varepsilon>0$, $t_j\to0$ and
$\varphi_j\in\mathcal K$ with
$\gamma_\ast(t_j)\|S_\beta(t_j)\varphi_j\|_{L_\Psi}\ge\varepsilon$. Passing to a
subsequence, $\varphi_j\to\varphi\in\mathcal K$ in $L_\Phi$. For $s>0$, by
(b) and (a)(i),
\begin{align*}
\gamma_\ast(t_j)\|S_\beta(t_j)\varphi_j\|_{L_\Psi}
&\le\gamma_\ast(t_j)\|S_\beta(t_j)(\varphi_j-S_\beta(s)\varphi)\|_{L_\Psi}
+\gamma_\ast(t_j)\|S_\beta(s)S_\beta(t_j)\varphi\|_{L_\Psi}\\
&\le\tfrac{2\kappa}{b}\|\varphi_j-S_\beta(s)\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}
+\tfrac{2\kappa}{b}\,\frac{\gamma_\ast(t_j)}{\gamma_\ast(s)}\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}.
\end{align*}
Letting $j\to\infty$ and then $s\to0$, and using (a)(ii), we obtain
$\varepsilon\le0$, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem:kernel}(ii) and the generalised H\"older inequality,
\begin{align*}
\bigl|S_\beta(t)\varphi(x)\bigr|
&\le\int_{\mathbb R^d}p_\beta(x-y,t)|\varphi(y)|\,\mathrm{d} y
\le 2\,\bigl\|p_\beta(x-\cdot,t)\bigr\|_{L_{\Phi^*}}\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}\\
&=2\,\bigl\|p_\beta(\cdot,t)\bigr\|_{L_{\Phi^*}}\,\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi} ,
\end{align*}
where we used that $p_\beta(\cdot,t)$ is even. By Corollary
\ref{cor:kernelTheta} with $\Theta=\Phi^*$ and the left inequality in
\eqref{eq:24},
$$
\bigl\|p_\beta(\cdot,t)\bigr\|_{L_{\Phi^*}}
\le \kappa\,\frac{t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}}{(\Phi^*)^{-1}(t^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}})}
\le \kappa\,\Phi^{-1}\bigl(t^{-\tfrac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr),
$$
which is \eqref{eq:MTtoLinfty} with $C_\infty=2\kappa$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Write $\alpha(t):=\Phi^{-1}(t^{-d/(2\beta)})$ and $\gamma(t):=1/\alpha(t)$, and
let $\mathcal K\subseteq L^0_\Phi$ be compact. Since $\Phi$ is finite,
$\Phi^{-1}$ is unbounded, and therefore $\gamma(t)\to0$ as $t\to0$. Suppose that there
are $\varepsilon>0$, $t_j\to0$ and $\varphi_j\in\mathcal K$ with
$\gamma(t_j)\|S_\beta(t_j)\varphi_j\|_{L^\infty}\ge\varepsilon$, and, passing to
a subsequence, $\varphi_j\to\varphi\in\mathcal K$ in $L_\Phi$. For $s>0$,
by \eqref{eq:MTtoLinfty}, the $L^\infty$-contractivity of $S_\beta(t_j)$
(Lemma~\ref{L5.1} with $\Phi=\Phi^\infty$) and \eqref{E5.2},
\begin{align*}
\gamma(t_j)\|S_\beta(t_j)\varphi_j\|_{L^\infty}
&\le\gamma(t_j)\bigl\|S_\beta(t_j)\bigl(\varphi_j-S_\beta(s)\varphi\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^\infty}
+\gamma(t_j)\bigl\|S_\beta(t_j)S_\beta(s)\varphi\bigr\|_{L^\infty}\\
&\le C_\infty\|\varphi_j-\varphi\|_{L_\Phi}
+C_\infty\bigl\|\varphi-S_\beta(s)\varphi\bigr\|_{L_\Phi}
+\gamma(t_j)\bigl\|S_\beta(s)\varphi\bigr\|_{L^\infty} .
\end{align*}
The last term tends to $0$ as $j\to\infty$ because
$\|S_\beta(s)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}<\infty$ for fixed $s>0$. Letting $j\to\infty$
and then $s\to0$, and using Theorem \ref{thm:OrliczSmoothing}(a)(ii), we obtain
$\varepsilon\le0$, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Let $\theta\in(0,1)$ and $t>0$. We use the semigroup property,
Lemma~\ref{L5.1} applied with $\Phi^\infty$ (that is, on $L^\infty$) and with
$m=m_\beta((1-\theta)t)$, and Corollary \ref{cor:Linfty}. We obtain
$$
\|S_\beta(t)\varphi\|_{L^\infty}
\le m_\beta((1-\theta)t)\,\bigl\|S_\beta(\theta t)\varphi\bigr\|_{L^\infty}
\le 2^{\frac d2}e^{-(1-\theta)\lambda_\beta t}\,C_\infty\,
\Phi^{-1}\bigl((\theta t)^{-\frac{d}{2\beta}}\bigr)\|\varphi\|_{L_\Phi} ,
$$
and $\Phi^{-1}((\theta t)^{-d/(2\beta)})\le\theta^{-d/(2\beta)}
\Phi^{-1}(t^{-d/(2\beta)})$ by \eqref{eq:dilate}. This is \eqref{eq:largetime}.
For $t\ge1$, the same argument with the splitting $t=(t-1)+1$ gives
\eqref{eq:largetime-sharp}. For $L_\Psi$, one argues in the same way, using
Lemma~\ref{L5.1} on $L_\Psi$ and \eqref{eq:OrliczOrlicz}, together with
$\Phi^{-1}(\theta^{-a}x)/\Psi^{-1}(\theta^{-a}x)\le\theta^{-a}\Phi^{-1}(x)/\Psi^{-1}(x)$,
$a=d/(2\beta)$, which follows from \eqref{eq:dilate} and the monotonicity of
$\Psi^{-1}$. The optimality of the rate follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}(iv), since $h_0\in L_\Phi\cap L^\infty$.

We turn to Proposition \ref{prop:LpLq}. For $q=r$, the estimate is
\eqref{E5.2}, since
$L^q=L_\Phi$ with $\Phi(x)=x^q/q$ and $\|\cdot\|_{L_\Phi}=q^{-1/q}\|\cdot\|_{L^q}$
(for $q=\infty$, use $\Phi^\infty$). For $q<r<\infty$, it is Theorem
\ref{thm:OrliczSmoothing}(b) with the choice of Remark \ref{rem:onLpLq}, and
for $r=\infty$, it is Corollary \ref{cor:Linfty} with $\Phi(x)=x^q/q$, for which
$\Phi^{-1}(x)=(qx)^{1/q}$. The exponential improvements follow from the first
part of the proof.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:restart}
Let $u$ be an integral solution of \eqref{eq:main} in $Q_T$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For a.e.\ $s\in(0,T)$, the function $u(s+\cdot)$ is an integral solution
in $Q_{T-s}$ with data $u(s)$.
\item If $s$ is as in \textup{(i)} and $v$ is an integral solution in $Q_\tau$
with data $u(s)$, then the function equal to $u$ on $\mathbb R^d\times(0,s]$ and to
$v(\cdot-s)$ on $\mathbb R^d\times(s,s+\tau)$ is an integral solution in $Q_{s+\tau}$
with data $u(0)$.
\item If $u$ is a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution on $[0,T)$, then
$u(t)=\mathcal F(u;u_0)(t)$ a.e.\ in $\mathbb R^d$ for \emph{every} $t\in(0,T)$, and
\textup{(i)} holds for every $s\in(0,T)$; the restarted function
$u(s+\cdot)$ is a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution on $[0,T-s)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
(i) Let $s$ be such that $u(s)=\mathcal F(u;u_0)(s)$ a.e.\ and the integrals
defining $\mathcal F(u;u_0)(s)$ converge absolutely a.e.; this holds for
a.e.\ $s$. By the semigroup property and Fubini's theorem (the integrals being
absolutely convergent for a.e.\ $(x,t)$),
$$
\mathcal F(u;u_0)(s+t)=S_\beta(t)\Bigl[S_\beta(s)u_0+\int_0^sS_\beta(s-r)f(u(r))
\,\mathrm{d} r\Bigr]+\int_0^tS_\beta(t-r)f(u(s+r))\,\mathrm{d} r ,
$$
where the bracket is $u(s)$, and $S_\beta(t)|u(s)|\le S_\beta(s+t)|u_0|+
\int_0^sS_\beta(s+t-r)|f(u(r))|\,\mathrm{d} r<\infty$ a.e.

(ii) This is proved by the same computation, read backwards.

(iii) Let $t\in(0,T)$, choose $t_0\in(0,t)$ as in
(i), and let $M$ bound $|u|$ on $\mathbb R^d\times(t_0,t+\varepsilon)$. For $\tau$
near $t$, (i) gives $u(\tau)=S_\beta(\tau-t_0)u(t_0)+D(\tau)$ with
$D(\tau):=\int_{t_0}^\tau S_\beta(\tau-r)f(u(r))\,\mathrm{d} r$ for a.e.\ such $\tau$.
Since $\|f(u(r))\|_\Phi\le\ell(M)\|u(r)\|_\Phi$ is bounded, the argument given
in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:local}(a) below shows that $\tau\mapsto D(\tau)$
is continuous into $L_\Phi$; so is $\tau\mapsto S_\beta(\tau-t_0)u(t_0)$.
As $u\in C([0,T),M_\Phi)$, the identity extends to $\tau=t$. By the computation
in (i), the right-hand side at $\tau=t$ coincides with $\mathcal F(u;u_0)(t)$.
The remaining assertions follow.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:Linfty}
Let $\psi\in M_\Phi\cap L^\infty$ with $\|\psi\|_\infty\le M$, and let
$\tau_M>0$ satisfy $\tau_M\,\ell(2M)\le\frac12$. Then \eqref{eq:main} with data
$\psi$ has a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution on $[0,\tau_M]$ with
$\|u(t)\|_\infty\le2M$. Moreover, two integral solutions in $Q_\tau$ with the
same data which both belong to $L^\infty(Q_\tau)$ coincide.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Let $X$ be the set of measurable $u$ on $Q_{\tau_M}$ with $|u|\le2M$ and
$\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_t\|u(t)\|_\Phi\le2\|\psi\|_\Phi$, with the metric of
$L^\infty(Q_{\tau_M})$; it is complete by Lemma~\ref{lem:orliczbasic}(i). For
$u\in X$, we infer from \eqref{E5.2}, its $L^\infty$ analogue, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:minkowski} that
\begin{gather*}
\|\mathcal F(u;\psi)(t)\|_\infty\le M+\tau_M\ell(2M)2M\le2M,\\
\|\mathcal F(u;\psi)(t)\|_\Phi\le\|\psi\|_\Phi+\tau_M\ell(2M)2\|\psi\|_\Phi
\le2\|\psi\|_\Phi,\\
\|\mathcal F(u;\psi)-\mathcal F(v;\psi)\|_{L^\infty(Q_{\tau_M})}\le\tfrac12
\|u-v\|_{L^\infty(Q_{\tau_M})}.
\end{gather*} The fixed point is a bounded integral
solution, and its continuity with values in $M_\Phi$ is proved as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:local}(a) below. Finally, if $v_1,v_2$ are two integral
solutions in $Q_\tau$ bounded by $M'$, then
$\|v_1(t)-v_2(t)\|_\infty\le\ell(M')\int_0^t\|v_1(s)-v_2(s)\|_\infty\,\mathrm{d} s$, and
we conclude from Lemma~\ref{lem:gronwall} that $v_1=v_2$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal K\subseteq M_\Phi$ be compact. By the lattice property,
$\||\phi|-|\psi|\|_\Phi\le\|\phi-\psi\|_\Phi$, so that
$|\mathcal K|:=\{|\psi|:\psi\in\mathcal K\}$ is compact in $M_\Phi=L^0_\Phi$.
By Corollary \ref{cor:Linftyzero} there exists $\tau_{\mathcal K}>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:barrierinfty}
\bigl\|2S_\beta(s)|\psi|\bigr\|_{\infty}\le\lambda\,\alpha(s),
\qquad s\in(0,\tau_{\mathcal K}),\ \psi\in\mathcal K .
\end{equation}
Choose $T=T_{\mathcal K}\in(0,\tau_{\mathcal K})$ with $I(T)\le\frac12$; this
choice does not depend on $u_0\in\mathcal K$. Fix $u_0\in\mathcal K$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:barrier}
w(t):=2S_\beta(t)|u_0|,\qquad t\ge0 ,
\end{equation}
and let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:space}
\begin{gathered}
X_{u_0}:=\Bigl\{u:Q_T\to\mathbb R\ \text{measurable}:\
|u|\le w\ \text{a.e.\ in } Q_T\Bigr\},\\
d(u,v):=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{t\in(0,T)}\|u(t)-v(t)\|_{\Phi}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Since $\|w(t)\|_\Phi\le2\|u_0\|_\Phi$ by \eqref{E5.2}, Lemma~\ref{lem:complete}
shows that $(X_{u_0},d)$ is a nonempty complete metric space. Note that
$u(t)\in M_\Phi$ for every $u\in X_{u_0}$ and a.e.\ $t$, since $w(t)\in
M_\Phi$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:OrliczSmoothing}(a)(i).

We first show that $\mathcal F=\mathcal F(\cdot\,;u_0)$ maps $X_{u_0}$ into
itself. Let $u\in X_{u_0}$. By \eqref{eq:barrierinfty}, $|u(x,s)|\le
w(x,s)\le\lambda\alpha(s)$ for a.e.\ $(x,s)\in Q_T$, and \eqref{eq:lipbound}
yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fbound}
|f(u(s))|\le\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)\,|u(s)|
\le\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)\,w(s)\qquad\text{a.e.\ in } Q_T .
\end{equation}
Since $S_\beta(t-s)$ has a nonnegative kernel and $S_\beta(t-s)w(s)
=2S_\beta(t-s)S_\beta(s)|u_0|=w(t)$, we obtain, for every $t\in(0,T)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:selfmap}
\bigl|\mathcal F(u)(t)\bigr|
\le S_\beta(t)|u_0|+\int_0^t\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)S_\beta(t-s)w(s)\,\mathrm{d} s
=\frac{w(t)}{2}+w(t)\,I(t)\ \le\ w(t) .
\end{equation}
In particular all integrals in $\mathcal F(u)$ converge absolutely a.e., and
$\mathcal F(u)$ is measurable by Tonelli's theorem. Hence $\mathcal
F(X_{u_0})\subseteq X_{u_0}$.

Next, we show that $\mathcal F$ is a contraction. Let $u,v\in X_{u_0}$ and
$t\in(0,T)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:minkowski}, \eqref{E5.2}, \eqref{eq:lipbound}
and \eqref{eq:barrierinfty}, we get
\begin{equation}\label{eq:contraction}
\begin{aligned}
\bigl\|\mathcal F(u)(t)-\mathcal F(v)(t)\bigr\|_{\Phi}
&\le\int_0^t\bigl\|S_\beta(t-s)\bigl|f(u(s))-f(v(s))\bigr|\bigr\|_{\Phi}\,\mathrm{d} s\\
&\le\int_0^t\bigl\|f(u(s))-f(v(s))\bigr\|_{\Phi}\,\mathrm{d} s\\
&\le\int_0^t\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)\|u(s)-v(s)\|_{\Phi}\,\mathrm{d} s
\ \le\ I(T)\,d(u,v)\le\tfrac12\,d(u,v).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Banach's fixed point theorem provides a unique $u=u(\cdot\,;u_0)\in X_{u_0}$
with $\mathcal F(u)=u$ a.e. We use the representative
$u(t):=\mathcal F(u)(t)$, which is defined for every $t\in(0,T)$ and satisfies
$|u(t)|\le w(t)$ by \eqref{eq:selfmap}. Thus $u$ is an integral solution in
$Q_T$, with $T=T_{\mathcal K}$ independent of $u_0\in\mathcal K$.

\emph{Boundedness and \eqref{eq:smallrate}.} By \eqref{eq:barrierinfty}, we
have $\|u(t)\|_\infty\le\|w(t)\|_\infty\le\lambda\alpha(t)$. Hence $u\in
L^\infty_{\rm loc}((0,T),L^\infty)$, and \eqref{eq:smallrate} follows from
$|u(t)|\le2S_\beta(t)|u_0|$ and Corollary \ref{cor:Linftyzero} applied to the
compact set $|\mathcal K|$.

\emph{Continuity in $M_\Phi$.} By Theorem~\ref{thm:OrliczSmoothing}(a)(iii),
the map $t\mapsto S_\beta(t)u_0$ belongs to $C([0,\infty),M_\Phi)$. It
therefore suffices to consider $D(t):=\int_0^tS_\beta(t-s)f(u(s))\,\mathrm{d} s$. By \eqref{eq:fbound},
$f(u(s))\in M_\Phi$ with
$\|f(u(s))\|_\Phi\le\ell(\lambda\alpha(s))\|w(s)\|_\Phi\le2\|u_0\|_\Phi\,
\ell(\lambda\alpha(s))=:g(s)$, and $g\in L^1(0,T)$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:minkowski} and \eqref{E5.2}, $\|D(t)\|_\Phi\le\int_0^tg\to0$ as
$t\to0$. For $0<t_0<t_1<T$, the semigroup property gives
$$
D(t_1)-D(t_0)=\int_0^{t_0}\bigl(S_\beta(t_1-t_0)-I\bigr)S_\beta(t_0-s)f(u(s))\,\mathrm{d} s
+\int_{t_0}^{t_1}S_\beta(t_1-s)f(u(s))\,\mathrm{d} s ,
$$
whence, by Lemma~\ref{lem:minkowski} and \eqref{E5.2},
$$
\|D(t_1)-D(t_0)\|_\Phi\le\int_0^{t_0}\bigl\|\bigl(S_\beta(t_1-t_0)-I\bigr)
f(u(s))\bigr\|_\Phi\,\mathrm{d} s+\int_{t_0}^{t_1}g(s)\,\mathrm{d} s ,
$$
where we used that $S_\beta(t_0-s)$ commutes with $S_\beta(t_1-t_0)-I$ and is
a contraction. Let $t_1-t_0\to0$, with either $t_0$ or $t_1$ fixed. By the
strong continuity of $S_\beta$ on $M_\Phi$, the integrand of the first
integral tends to $0$ for a.e.\ $s$, and it is dominated by $2g(s)$. By
dominated convergence, both integrals tend to $0$. Hence $D\in
C([0,T),M_\Phi)$, and $u$ is a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution.

\emph{Sign.} Let $f$ be positone and $u_0\ge0$. The set
$X^+_{u_0}:=\{u\in X_{u_0}:u\ge0\}$ is nonempty and closed in $(X_{u_0},d)$,
since the limit in Lemma~\ref{lem:complete} is an a.e.\ limit. It is also
invariant under $\mathcal F$, because $f\ge0$ on $[0,\infty)$. Consequently,
the fixed point lies in $X^+_{u_0}$. The case $u_0\le0$ is symmetric.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal K\subseteq M_\Phi$ be compact, let $\phi,\varphi\in\mathcal K$ and
write $u:=u(\cdot\,;\phi)$, $v:=u(\cdot\,;\varphi)$ for the solutions of part
(a), and $\tau_{\mathcal K}':=\min\{\tau_{\mathcal K},T_{\mathcal K}\}/2$. By
construction and \eqref{eq:barrierinfty},
$\|u(t)\|_\infty,\|v(t)\|_\infty\le\lambda\alpha(t)$ for $t\in(0,T_{\mathcal
K})$. Put
$$
y(t):=\|u(t)-v(t)\|_{\Phi}+\gamma(t)\,\|u(t)-v(t)\|_{\infty},
\qquad t\in(0,\tau'_{\mathcal K}] ;
$$
$y$ is measurable by Lemma~\ref{lem:minkowski}. It is also bounded, since
$\|u(t)-v(t)\|_\Phi\le2(\|\phi\|_\Phi+\|\varphi\|_\Phi)$ and
$\gamma(t)\|u(t)-v(t)\|_\infty\le2\lambda$. Arguing as in
\eqref{eq:contraction}, but keeping the initial data, and using \eqref{E5.2}, we get
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cd1}
\|u(t)-v(t)\|_{\Phi}\le\|\phi-\varphi\|_{\Phi}
+\int_0^t\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)\|u(s)-v(s)\|_{\Phi}\,\mathrm{d} s .
\end{equation}
For the $L^\infty$ part, we split the Duhamel integral at $t/2$. On $(t/2,t)$,
we use the $L^\infty$-contractivity of $S_\beta$; on $(0,t/2)$, we use
\eqref{eq:MTtoLinfty} together with $\alpha(t-s)\le\alpha(t/2)\le
2^{d/(2\beta)}\alpha(t)$ for $s\le t/2$:
\begin{align*}
\|u(t)-v(t)\|_{\infty}
&\le C_\infty\alpha(t)\|\phi-\varphi\|_{\Phi}
+\int_{t/2}^{t}\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)\|u(s)-v(s)\|_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d} s\\
&\qquad
+C_\infty2^{\frac{d}{2\beta}}\alpha(t)\int_0^{t/2}\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)
\|u(s)-v(s)\|_{\Phi}\,\mathrm{d} s .
\end{align*}
Multiplying by $\gamma(t)$ and noting that $\gamma(t)\le2^{d/(2\beta)}\gamma(s)$
for $s\in[t/2,t]$, again by \eqref{eq:alphadouble}, we arrive at
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cd2}
\gamma(t)\|u(t)-v(t)\|_{\infty}
\le C_\infty\|\phi-\varphi\|_{\Phi}
+C'\int_0^{t}\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)\,y(s)\,\mathrm{d} s,
\qquad C':=\max\{1,C_\infty\}2^{\frac{d}{2\beta}} .
\end{equation}
We add \eqref{eq:cd1} and \eqref{eq:cd2}, and apply Lemma~\ref{lem:gronwall}
on $(0,\tau'_{\mathcal K}]$ with $k(s)=(1+C')\ell(\lambda\alpha(s))$. We obtain
\eqref{eq:contdep} with $\tau_{\mathcal K}$ replaced by $\tau'_{\mathcal K}$ and
$C_{\mathcal K}:=(1+C_\infty)\exp\bigl((1+C')I(\tau'_{\mathcal K})\bigr)$. (The
inequality holds for a.e.\ $t$, and then for every $t$ by the continuity of
$t\mapsto u(t)-v(t)$ in $L_\Phi$ and the lower semicontinuity of
$\|\cdot\|_\infty$ under a.e.\ convergence.) Renaming $\tau'_{\mathcal K}$ as
$\tau_{\mathcal K}$ proves (c).
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
We follow \cite[Section 5.3]{Laister}, which in turn adapts
\cite{brezis1996nonlinear,LaisterSierzega2020}. Let $v$ be a bounded
$M_\Phi$-mild solution on $[0,T)$ with $v(0)=u_0$, and fix $T_1\in(0,T)$. The set
$\mathcal K':=v([0,T_1])$ is compact in $M_\Phi$; let $T_{\mathcal K'}$,
$\tau_{\mathcal K'}$ and $C_{\mathcal K'}$ be given by (a) and (c). Let
$s\in(0,T_1)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}(iii), $v(s+\cdot)$ is a bounded
$M_\Phi$-mild solution with data $v(s)\in\mathcal K'\cap L^\infty$, while
$u(\cdot\,;v(s))$ is a solution with the same data satisfying
$|u(t;v(s))|\le2S_\beta(t)|v(s)|\le2\|v(s)\|_\infty$. Both are bounded on
$\mathbb R^d\times(0,\tau')$ for every $\tau'<\min\{T_{\mathcal K'},T_1-s\}$, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:Linfty} gives
$$
v(s+t)=u(t;v(s)),\qquad 0\le t<\min\{T_{\mathcal K'},T_1-s\}.
$$
Fix $t\in(0,\min\{\tau_{\mathcal K'},T_1\})$ and let $s\downarrow0$. Then
$v(s+t)\to v(t)$ in $M_\Phi$ by continuity of $v$, while
$\|u(t;v(s))-u(t;u_0)\|_\Phi\le C_{\mathcal K'}\|v(s)-u_0\|_\Phi\to0$ by (c).
Hence $v(t)=u(t;u_0)$ for $0\le t\le t_1:=\frac12\min\{\tau_{\mathcal
K'},T_1\}$. On the remaining interval, both functions are integral solutions
starting from $v(t_1)$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}(iii)), and they are bounded on
compact subintervals. Lemma~\ref{lem:Linfty} then yields $v=u(\cdot;u_0)$ on
their common interval of existence.

Uniqueness allows us to glue solutions defined on overlapping intervals. We
let $T_{u_0}\in(0,\infty]$ be the supremum of all $T$ for which a bounded
$M_\Phi$-mild solution exists on $[0,T)$. Suppose that $T_{u_0}<\infty$ and that
there are $M<\infty$ and $t_j\uparrow T_{u_0}$ with $\|u(t_j)\|_\infty\le M$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:Linfty} there is a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution with data
$u(t_j)$ on $[0,\tau_M]$, with $\tau_M$ independent of $j$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}(ii),(iii), its concatenation with $u$ is a bounded
$M_\Phi$-mild solution on $[0,t_j+\tau_M)$. Since $t_j+\tau_M>T_{u_0}$ for $j$
large, this is a contradiction. Hence $\|u(t)\|_\infty\to\infty$ as $t\uparrow
T_{u_0}$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Let $f$ be increasing, $\phi\le\varphi$ in $M_\Phi$, $u:=u(\cdot\,;\phi)$,
$v:=u(\cdot\,;\varphi)$ and $z:=u-v$. Since $f$ is increasing, for $|a|,|b|\le
s$ we have $f(a)-f(b)\le\ell(s)(a-b)^+$. Apply (a) to the compact set
$\{\phi,\varphi\}$: for $0<t<T_0:=T_{\{\phi,\varphi\}}$, both solutions are
bounded by $\lambda\alpha(t)$. Since $S_\beta(t)(\phi-\varphi)\le0$, we get
$$
z(t)\le\int_0^tS_\beta(t-s)\bigl(f(u(s))-f(v(s))\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} s
\le\int_0^t\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)S_\beta(t-s)z^+(s)\,\mathrm{d} s .
$$
The right-hand side is nonnegative, so $z^+(t)$ obeys the same bound. Then
Lemma~\ref{lem:minkowski} and \eqref{E5.2} give
$\|z^+(t)\|_\Phi\le\int_0^t\ell(\lambda\alpha(s))\|z^+(s)\|_\Phi\,\mathrm{d} s$. As
$\|z^+(t)\|_\Phi$ is bounded, Lemma~\ref{lem:gronwall} yields $z^+=0$ on
$(0,T_0)$. Now let $t_0\in(0,T_0)$ and $T'<\min\{T_\phi,T_\varphi\}$, and let
$M$ bound $|u|$ and $|v|$ on $\mathbb R^d\times(t_0,T')$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}(iii), for $t\in(t_0,T')$,
$z(t)\le S_\beta(t-t_0)z(t_0)+\ell(M)\int_{t_0}^tS_\beta(t-s)z^+(s)\,\mathrm{d} s$ with
$z(t_0)\le0$. Hence $\|z^+(t)\|_\infty\le\ell(M)\int_{t_0}^t\|z^+(s)\|_\infty\,\mathrm{d} s$,
and we conclude from Lemma~\ref{lem:gronwall} that $z^+=0$ on $(t_0,T')$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
\rm
The comparison principle can also be obtained, as in
\cite[Section 5.4]{Laister}, by monotone iteration from the super- and
subsolutions $\pm2S_\beta(t)\phi^\pm$. The direct argument above avoids the
passage to the limit in the iteration.
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Let $u_0\in M_\Phi$ with $\|u_0\|_\Phi\le\lambda/(AC_\infty)$ and put
$w(t):=A\,S_\beta(t)|u_0|$. By \eqref{eq:MTtoLinfty},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:globalbarrier}
\|w(s)\|_{\infty}\le AC_\infty\,\alpha(s)\,\|u_0\|_{\Phi}\le\lambda\,\alpha(s),
\qquad s>0 .
\end{equation}
Let $X^\infty_{u_0}$ be the set of measurable $u$ on $Q_\infty$ with $|u|\le w$,
equipped with the metric $d_\infty(u,v):=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{t>0}\|u(t)-v(t)\|_\Phi$.
Since $\|w(t)\|_\Phi\le A\|u_0\|_\Phi$, it is a nonempty complete metric space
by Lemma~\ref{lem:complete}. For $u\in X^\infty_{u_0}$ we have
$|f(u(s))|\le\ell(\lambda\alpha(s))w(s)$ by \eqref{eq:lipbound} and
\eqref{eq:globalbarrier}, whence, using $S_\beta(t-s)w(s)=w(t)$ and
\eqref{eq:changevar} with $T=\infty$,
$$
\bigl|\mathcal F(u)(t)\bigr|
\le S_\beta(t)|u_0|+\int_0^{t}\ell\bigl(\lambda\alpha(s)\bigr)
S_\beta(t-s)w(s)\,\mathrm{d} s
\le\frac{w(t)}{A}+w(t)\,I_0=w(t),\qquad t>0 ,
$$
by the definition of $A$. Thus $\mathcal F$ maps $X^\infty_{u_0}$ into itself.
The computation \eqref{eq:contraction}, performed on $(0,\infty)$, gives
$d_\infty(\mathcal F(u),\mathcal F(v))\le I_0\,d_\infty(u,v)$ with $I_0<1$.
Hence $\mathcal F$ has a unique fixed point $u\in X^\infty_{u_0}$. Exactly as in the
proof of Theorem \ref{thm:local}(a) (with $g(s)=A\|u_0\|_\Phi\ell(\lambda\alpha(s))
\in L^1(0,\infty)$), $u$ is a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution on $[0,\infty)$,
and it is unique by Theorem \ref{thm:local}(b).

Estimate \eqref{eq:globaldecay1} follows from $|u(t)|\le w(t)$ and
\eqref{eq:globalbarrier}. Since $\Phi$ is positive, $\Phi^{-1}(x)\to0$ as
$x\downarrow0$, and therefore $\alpha(t)\to0$ as $t\to\infty$. Estimates
\eqref{eq:globaldecay2} and \eqref{eq:expdecay} follow from $|u(t)|\le
AS_\beta(t)|u_0|$, \eqref{E5.2} and \eqref{eq:largetime-sharp}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Put $C_\ast:=C_\infty2^{d/2}e^{\lambda_\beta}\Phi^{-1}(1)$ and, for $\delta>0$,
$$
\omega_\delta(s):=
\begin{cases}
2C_\infty\delta\,\alpha(s), & 0<s<1,\\
2C_\ast\delta\,e^{-\lambda_\beta s}, & s\ge1,
\end{cases}
\qquad
J(\delta):=\int_0^\infty\ell\bigl(\omega_\delta(s)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} s .
$$
The Dini condition \eqref{eq:dini} forces $\ell(0^+)=0$; otherwise, we would
have $\int_0^1\ell(r)\,\,\mathrm{d} r/r\ge\ell(0^+)\int_0^1\,\mathrm{d} r/r=\infty$. The substitution
$r=2C_\ast\delta e^{-\lambda_\beta s}$ gives
$$
J(\delta)=\int_0^1\ell\bigl(2C_\infty\delta\,\alpha(s)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} s
+\frac{1}{\lambda_\beta}\int_0^{2C_\ast\delta e^{-\lambda_\beta}}
\frac{\ell(r)}{r}\,\mathrm{d} r .
$$
For $2C_\infty\delta\le\lambda$ the first integrand is bounded by
$\ell(\lambda\alpha(s))$, which is integrable on $(0,1)$ by \eqref{eq:IC-infty}
and \eqref{eq:changevar}, and it tends to $\ell(0^+)=0$ as $\delta\to0$. The
second term tends to $0$ by \eqref{eq:dini}. Hence we may fix $\delta>0$ with
$2C_\infty\delta\le\lambda$ and $J(\delta)\le\frac12$.

Let $\|u_0\|_\Phi\le\delta$ and $w(t):=2S_\beta(t)|u_0|$. By
Corollary~\ref{cor:Linfty} for $s<1$ and \eqref{eq:largetime-sharp} for
$s\ge1$, we have $\|w(s)\|_\infty\le\omega_\delta(s)$ for all $s>0$. Consider
the complete metric space of measurable $u$ on $Q_\infty$ with $|u|\le w$,
equipped with $d_\infty$. The argument of the preceding proof gives
$|\mathcal F(u)(t)|\le\frac{w(t)}2+w(t)J(\delta)\le w(t)$ and
$d_\infty(\mathcal F(u),\mathcal F(v))\le J(\delta)d_\infty(u,v)\le\frac12
d_\infty(u,v)$. The fixed point is a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution on
$[0,\infty)$ (with $g(s)=2\delta\,\ell(\omega_\delta(s))\in L^1(0,\infty)$ in the
continuity argument), and it is unique by Theorem~\ref{thm:local}(b). The
estimates follow from $|u(t)|\le2S_\beta(t)|u_0|$, \eqref{E5.2} and
\eqref{eq:largetime-sharp}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:supersol}
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$, let $\mu\ge0$ be measurable and let
$U:Q_T\to[0,\infty)$ be measurable with
$S_\beta(t)\mu+\int_0^tS_\beta(t-s)f(U(s))\,\mathrm{d} s\le U(t)$ a.e.\ in $Q_T$. Then
\eqref{eq:main} with data $\mu$ has a nonnegative integral solution $u\le U$
in $Q_T$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Let $v_0:=0$ and $v_{k+1}:=\mathcal F(v_k;\mu)$. Since $f\ge0$ is increasing on
$[0,\infty)$ and the kernel is nonnegative, we get $0\le v_k\le v_{k+1}\le U$
a.e.\ by induction. The limit $u:=\lim_kv_k\le U$ is finite a.e., and the
monotone convergence theorem and the continuity of $f$ give $\mathcal
