F(u;\mu)=\lim_k\mathcal F(v_k;\mu)=u$, with absolutely convergent integrals.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- corollary-8: body_md -->
\label{cor:datacomp}
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$ and $0\le\mu_2\le\mu_1$. If \eqref{eq:main} has a
nonnegative integral solution in $Q_T$ with data $\mu_1$, then it has one with
data $\mu_2$.
<!-- end corollary-8 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
It suffices to apply Lemma~\ref{lem:supersol} with $\mu=\mu_2$ and with $U$
the solution for $\mu_1$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:freecomp}
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$ and $\mu\ge0$. If the free problem
$\partial_tu+(-\Delta)^\beta u=f(u)$ has a nonnegative integral solution $U$ in
$Q_T$ with data $\mu$ (defined with the kernel $p_\beta$), then
\eqref{eq:main} has a nonnegative integral solution $u\le U$ in $Q_T$ with
data $\mu$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Since $0\le\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(x,y)\le p_\beta(x-y,t)$ and all integrands are
nonnegative, we have $S_\beta(t)\mu+\int_0^tS_\beta(t-s)f(U(s))\,\mathrm{d} s\le U(t)$.
The claim follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:supersol}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{L7.2}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$ with $F(u)<\infty$ for
$u>0$, and let $u_0\ge0$ be measurable and not a.e.\ zero. If \eqref{eq:main}
possesses a nonnegative integral solution in $Q_T$, then for a.e.\
$T'\in(0,T)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:necessary}
F\bigl([S_\beta(T')u_0](x)\bigr)\ \ge\ T'
\qquad\text{for a.e. }x\in\mathbb R^d .
\end{equation}
If moreover $u_0\in L^1$, then $F([S_\beta(t)u_0](x))\ge t$ for every
$x\in\mathbb R^d$ and every $t\in(0,T)$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
\emph{Jensen's inequality for $S_\beta$.} Let $v\ge0$ be measurable. Since
$f$ is convex with $f(0)=0$ and
$M^\beta_\tau(x):=\int\mathcal{K}^\beta_\tau(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} y\le1$ by Lemma
\ref{lem:kernel}(iii), we have $f(z)\le M^\beta_\tau(x)f(z/M_\tau^\beta(x))$ for
$z\ge0$. Moreover, Jensen's inequality for the probability measure
$\mathcal{K}^\beta_\tau(x,y)\,\mathrm{d} y/M^\beta_\tau(x)$ gives
$f\bigl(M_\tau^\beta(x)^{-1}[S_\beta(\tau)v](x)\bigr)\le
M^\beta_\tau(x)^{-1}[S_\beta(\tau)f(v)](x)$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:jensen}
f\bigl([S_\beta(\tau)v](x)\bigr)\ \le\ [S_\beta(\tau)f(v)](x),
\qquad \tau>0,\ x\in\mathbb R^d ,
\end{equation}
with the convention $f(\infty)=\infty$.

\emph{A differential inequality.} Let $u\ge0$ be an integral solution in
$Q_T$. By Fubini's theorem, for a.e.\ $T'\in(0,T)$, the function
$\mathcal F(u;u_0)(T')$ coincides a.e.\ with $u(T')$ and is therefore finite
a.e. Fix such a $T'$ and let $\Omega:=\{x:\mathcal F(u;u_0)(T')(x)<\infty\}$,
a set of full measure. For $s\in(0,T')$ put
$$
U(s):=S_\beta(T')u_0+\int_0^sS_\beta(T'-\tau)f(u(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau\ \in[0,\infty].
$$
By the semigroup property and Tonelli's theorem,
$U(s)=S_\beta(T'-s)\mathcal F(u;u_0)(s)$. Since $\mathcal F(u;u_0)(s)=u(s)$
a.e.\ for a.e.\ $s$, we deduce that $U(s)=S_\beta(T'-s)u(s)$ everywhere in $\mathbb R^d$ for
a.e.\ $s\in(0,T')$. Moreover $s\mapsto U(s)(x)$ is nondecreasing, and
$U(s)\le\mathcal F(u;u_0)(T')$, so that $U(s)(x)<\infty$ for $x\in\Omega$. For
$x\in\Omega$ and $0<s<t<T'$, \eqref{eq:jensen} with $v=u(\tau)$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:semigroupid}
U(t)(x)=U(s)(x)+\int_s^t[S_\beta(T'-\tau)f(u(\tau))](x)\,\mathrm{d}\tau
\ \ge\ U(s)(x)+\int_s^tf\bigl(U(\tau)(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\tau .
\end{equation}
Since $u_0\ge0$ is not a.e.\ zero and $\mathcal{K}^\beta_{T'}>0$, we also have
$U(s)(x)\ge[S_\beta(T')u_0](x)>0$.

\emph{Integration.} Fix $x\in\Omega$ and $s\in(0,T')$, and set
$G(t):=U(s)(x)+\int_s^tf(U(\tau)(x))\,\mathrm{d}\tau$ for $t\in[s,T')$. Since
$0\le f(U(\tau)(x))\le f(\mathcal F(u;u_0)(T')(x))<\infty$, $G$ is finite,
nondecreasing and absolutely continuous, with $0<G\le U(\cdot)(x)$ by
\eqref{eq:semigroupid}. Moreover, $G'=f(U(\cdot)(x))\ge f(G)$ a.e., since $f$
is increasing. Consequently
$$
-\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d} t}F\bigl(G(t)\bigr)=\frac{G'(t)}{f(G(t))}\ \ge\ 1
\qquad\text{for a.e. }t\in(s,T'),
$$
Integrating from $s$ to $t$ and using $F>0$, we obtain
$F(U(s)(x))=F(G(s))\ge t-s+F(G(t))>t-s$. Letting $t\uparrow T'$, we get
$F(U(s)(x))\ge T'-s$. Since $U(s)\ge S_\beta(T')u_0$ and $F$ is decreasing, it
follows that $F([S_\beta(T')u_0](x))\ge T'-s$. Letting $s\downarrow0$ proves
\eqref{eq:necessary} for $x\in\Omega$.

If $u_0\in L^1$, then $(x,t)\mapsto[S_\beta(t)u_0](x)$ is continuous and
positive on $\mathbb R^d\times(0,\infty)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}(v), and $F$ is
continuous on $(0,\infty)$. Since \eqref{eq:necessary} holds on a dense subset
of $\mathbb R^d\times(0,T)$, we conclude that it holds everywhere.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:Fprops}
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$ and $(\mathrm G)$. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $F$ is rapidly varying of index $-\infty$ at infinity, and $F^{-1}$ is
slowly varying at $0^{+}$;
\item for every $q>1$ the function $g_q(\sigma):=F^{-1}(\sigma^{q})$ is
decreasing on $(0,\infty)$ and there is $\sigma_*=\sigma_*(q)>0$ such that
$g_q$ is convex on $(0,\sigma_*)$;
\item $f(u)/u\to\infty$ as $u\to\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
By \cite[Lemma 6.1(i)--(ii)]{Laister}, $\log f$ is regularly varying of index
$\rho\in(0,\infty]$, and $u^{-k}f(u)\to\infty$ for every $k>0$. The latter
property gives (iii). For $\lambda>1$, $\log f(\lambda u)-\log f(u)=\log
f(u)\bigl(\frac{\log f(\lambda u)}{\log f(u)}-1\bigr)\to\infty$, since $\log
f(u)\to\infty$ and the ratio tends to $\lambda^\rho>1$ (or to $\infty$). Thus
$f(\lambda u)/f(u)\to\infty$. Moreover $(\log f)'=f'/f$ is regularly varying
of index $\rho-1$, so $(f'/f)(\lambda u)/(f'/f)(u)$ tends to
$\lambda^{\rho-1}>0$ (or to $\infty$ if $\rho=\infty$). Hence
$f'(\lambda u)/f'(u)\to\infty$ for $\lambda>1$, and consequently
$f'(\lambda u)/f'(u)\to0$ for $\lambda<1$. By \eqref{eq:limitone}, $F\sim1/f'$
at infinity, whence $F(\lambda u)/F(u)\to0$ for $\lambda>1$ and $\to\infty$ for
$\lambda<1$. In other words, $F$ is rapidly varying of index $-\infty$.

For the second half of (i), fix $\lambda>1$ and let $\sigma\to0^{+}$. Writing
$u:=F^{-1}(\sigma)\to\infty$ and $c_\sigma:=F^{-1}(\lambda\sigma)/F^{-1}(\sigma)
\in(0,1]$, \eqref{eq:Finv} gives $F(c_\sigma u)=\lambda F(u)$. If
$\liminf_{\sigma\to0}c_\sigma=c<1$, pick $c'\in(c,1)$; along a sequence
$\sigma_j\to0$ with $c_{\sigma_j}\le c'$, we get $F(c_{\sigma_j}u)\ge F(c'u)$,
and therefore $\lambda\ge F(c'u)/F(u)\to\infty$, a contradiction. Hence
$c_\sigma\to1$. The case $\lambda<1$ follows by replacing $\sigma$ with
$\lambda^{-1}\sigma$. Hence $F^{-1}$ is slowly varying at $0^{+}$.

(ii) $g_q$ is decreasing because $F^{-1}$ is. By \eqref{E7.3},
$g_q'(\sigma)=-q\sigma^{q-1}f\bigl(F^{-1}(\sigma^{q})\bigr)$, which is $C^1$ since
$f\in C^1$. A further differentiation gives
$$
g_q''(\sigma)=q\,\sigma^{q-2}\,f\bigl(F^{-1}(\sigma^{q})\bigr)\,
\Bigl[q\,\sigma^{q}f'\bigl(F^{-1}(\sigma^{q})\bigr)-(q-1)\Bigr].
$$
By \eqref{E7.2}, the quantity $\sigma^{q}f'(F^{-1}(\sigma^{q}))$ tends to $1$
as $\sigma\to0^{+}$. Hence the bracket tends to $1$, and $g_q''>0$ on some
interval $(0,\sigma_*)$.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{L7.1}
Let $0<\beta\le1$, $\varrho>0$, and let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$ and
$(\mathrm G)$. For every $\gamma\in(0,\gamma_0(d,\beta))$ and every $R>0$,
problem \eqref{eq:main} possesses no nonnegative integral solution in $Q_T$,
for any $T>0$, with initial data
\begin{equation}\label{eq:data}
u_0(x)=F^{-1}\bigl(\gamma|x|^{2\beta}\bigr)\chi_{B_R}(x),
\qquad x\in\mathbb R^d\setminus\{0\} .
\end{equation}
Moreover $u_0\in L^1$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
\emph{Integrability.} By Lemma~\ref{lem:Fprops}(i), $r\mapsto
F^{-1}(\gamma r^{2\beta})$ is slowly varying at $0^+$, hence $o(r^{-1/2})$ as
$r\to0$ \cite[Proposition 1.3.6]{BGT}. We conclude that $u_0\in L^1$, because
$d-1-\frac12>-1$.

\emph{Reductions.} Fix $\gamma<\gamma_0(d,\beta)$ and choose $\alpha\in(0,2\beta)$
with $\gamma\,c_{d,\alpha,\beta}^{2\beta/\alpha}<1$. Set $q:=2\beta/\alpha>1$,
$c:=c_{d,\alpha,\beta}$ and $g:=g_q=F^{-1}(\cdot^{\,q})$, so that
$u_0(x)=g\bigl(\gamma^{1/q}|x|^{\alpha}\bigr)\chi_{B_R}(x)$. Let $\sigma_*$ be as
in Lemma \ref{lem:Fprops}(ii). By Corollary~\ref{cor:datacomp}, it suffices to
prove the claim for small $R$; we may thus assume $\gamma^{1/q}R^{\alpha}<\sigma_*$.
Suppose, for a contradiction, that a nonnegative integral solution exists in
$Q_T$. Since $u_0\in L^1$, Lemma \ref{L7.2} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nec0}
F\bigl([S_\beta(t)u_0](0)\bigr)\ge t,\qquad t\in(0,T).
\end{equation}

\emph{The mass defect.} Put $m(t):=\int_{B_R}\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(0,y)\,\mathrm{d} y\in(0,1]$. We
claim that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:massdefect}
1-m(t)\le C_R\,t,\qquad t>0,\qquad
C_R:=2\max\{1,d\varrho\}+2\max\{1,2d/R^2\}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, $1-m(t)=\bigl(1-\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(0,y)\,\mathrm{d} y\bigr)+\int_{|y|\ge
R}\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(0,y)\,\mathrm{d} y$. By \eqref{eq:mass1} with $y=0$, symmetry and
\eqref{E7.5}, the first term equals
$\int_0^\infty\bigl(1-(\cosh2\omega s)^{-d/2}\bigr)\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s$. Since
$\cosh z\le e^{z^2/2}$, we have $1-(\cosh z)^{-d/2}\le\min\{1,dz^2/4\}$. With
$z=2\omega s$, this is at most $\min\{1,d\varrho s^2\}\le\max\{1,d\varrho\}
\min\{1,s\}$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:stable}(iii) bounds the first term by
$2\max\{1,d\varrho\}t$. For the second term, Lemma \ref{lem:kernel}(ii) and
Chebyshev's inequality for the Gaussian $G(\cdot,s)$, whose second moment is
$2ds$, give
\begin{align*}
\int_{|y|\ge R}\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(0,y)\,\mathrm{d} y&\le\int_0^\infty\Bigl(\int_{|y|\ge R}G(y,s)
\,\mathrm{d} y\Bigr)\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s\\
&\le\int_0^\infty\min\Bigl\{1,\frac{2ds}{R^2}\Bigr\}\eta^\beta_t(s)\,\mathrm{d} s
\le2\max\Bigl\{1,\frac{2d}{R^2}\Bigr\}t ,
\end{align*}
again by Lemma~\ref{lem:stable}(iii). This proves \eqref{eq:massdefect}.

\emph{A lower bound at the origin.} Let $h(y):=\gamma^{1/q}|y|^{\alpha}$, and
let $\nu_t$ be the measure with density $\mathcal{K}^\beta_t(0,\cdot)\chi_{B_R}$,
whose total mass is $m(t)$. Since $h$ maps $B_R\setminus\{0\}$ into
$(0,\sigma_*)$, where $g$ is convex, Jensen's inequality for the probability
measure $\nu_t/m(t)$ yields
$$
[S_\beta(t)u_0](0)=\int g\circ h\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu_t
\ \ge\ m(t)\,g\Bigl(\frac{1}{m(t)}\int h\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu_t\Bigr).
$$
By Lemma \ref{lem:kernel}(ii) and the self-similarity of $p_\beta$,
$$
\int h\,\,\mathrm{d}\nu_t\le\gamma^{1/q}\int_{\mathbb R^d}p_\beta(y,t)|y|^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d} y
=c\,\gamma^{1/q}\,t^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}} .
$$
Let $\eta\in(0,1)$. For $0<t\le\eta/C_R$, we have $m(t)\ge1-\eta$ by
\eqref{eq:massdefect}. Since $g$ is decreasing and $\alpha q/(2\beta)=1$, we
obtain
$$
[S_\beta(t)u_0](0)\ \ge\ m(t)\,
g\Bigl(\frac{c}{1-\eta}\,\gamma^{1/q}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}\Bigr)
=m(t)\,F^{-1}\bigl(c_1\gamma t\bigr),
\qquad c_1:=\Bigl(\frac{c}{1-\eta}\Bigr)^{q} .
$$

\emph{Absorbing the mass defect.} Let $c_2>c_1$. We claim that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:absorb}
m(t)\,F^{-1}(c_1\gamma t)\ \ge\ F^{-1}(c_2\gamma t)
\qquad\text{for all small }t>0 .
\end{equation}
Writing $\sigma:=\gamma t$ and using \eqref{E7.3} together with the
monotonicity of $\tau\mapsto f(F^{-1}(\tau))$,
$$
F^{-1}(c_1\sigma)-F^{-1}(c_2\sigma)=\int_{c_1\sigma}^{c_2\sigma}
f\bigl(F^{-1}(\tau)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\tau\ \ge\ (c_2-c_1)\,\sigma\,
f\bigl(F^{-1}(c_2\sigma)\bigr) ,
$$
whence, with $W_i:=F^{-1}(c_i\sigma)$,
$$
1-\frac{W_2}{W_1}
\ \ge\ (c_2-c_1)\,\sigma\,\frac{f(W_2)}{W_2}\,\frac{W_2}{W_1} .
$$
By Lemma \ref{lem:Fprops}(i), $W_2/W_1\to1$ as $\sigma\to0^{+}$, and by
Lemma~\ref{lem:Fprops}(iii), $f(W_2)/W_2\to\infty$ since $W_2\to\infty$.
Hence, for $t$ small, the right-hand side exceeds $C_R\sigma/\gamma=C_Rt\ge
1-m(t)$, and \eqref{eq:absorb} follows.

\emph{Conclusion.} By \eqref{eq:nec0}, the two lower bounds and the
monotonicity of $F$, we get, for all small $t>0$,
$$
t\ \le\ F\bigl([S_\beta(t)u_0](0)\bigr)
\ \le\ F\bigl(F^{-1}(c_2\gamma t)\bigr)=c_2\gamma t ,
$$
so that $c_2\gamma\ge1$. However, $\gamma c^{q}<1$. We may therefore choose
$\eta$ so small, and then $c_2>c_1=(c/(1-\eta))^q$ so close to $c_1$, that
$c_2\gamma<1$, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{L7.3}
Under the hypotheses of Lemma \ref{L7.1}, for every $\gamma\in(0,\gamma_0)$
problem \eqref{eq:main} possesses no nonnegative integral solution with the
untruncated data $u_c(x)=F^{-1}(\gamma|x|^{2\beta})$, $x\ne0$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Assume that such a solution exists. Then Corollary~\ref{cor:datacomp}, with
$\mu_1=u_c$ and $\mu_2=u_c\chi_{B_R}$, contradicts Lemma~\ref{L7.1}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:onwhatisproved}
Lemmas \ref{L7.2}, \ref{lem:Fprops}, \ref{L7.1} and \ref{L7.3} are proved here
in full, for all $0<\beta\le1$ and all $\varrho>0$. The only external inputs
are \cite[Lemma 6.1(i)--(ii),(iv)]{Laister}. These are statements about
regularly varying functions, and they involve neither the equation nor the
operator.
In particular we do not appeal to \cite{Fujishima2024}, on which the
corresponding step of \cite[Section 6.3]{Laister} relies.

The threshold $\gamma_0(d,\beta)$ depends neither on $\varrho$ nor on $f$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:freecomp}, a nonnegative solution of the \emph{free} problem
$\partial_tu+(-\Delta)^\beta u=f(u)$ with data \eqref{eq:data} would produce
one of \eqref{eq:main}. Hence Lemma~\ref{L7.1} also yields nonexistence for
the free fractional heat equation with data \eqref{eq:data}, for every
$\gamma<\gamma_0(d,\beta)$. This gives an explicit value of the constant in the
necessity part of \cite[Theorem 1.1(a)]{Fujishima2024} for this profile (with
$\theta=2\beta$).

The exponent $2\beta$ in \eqref{eq:data} is the one for which the profile is
invariant under the parabolic scaling of the free fractional operator at small
scales: for $f(u)=e^u$, for instance, $F^{-1}(\gamma|x|^{2\beta})=
-\log\gamma-2\beta\log|x|$, and the equation
$\partial_tu+(-\Delta)^\beta u=e^u$ is invariant under
$u\mapsto u(\mu x,\mu^{2\beta}t)+2\beta\log\mu$. In the Jensen step of the
proof of Lemma~\ref{L7.1} it enters through the condition $\alpha q=2\beta$
with $\alpha<2\beta$, which reflects the fact that the $\beta$-stable
subordinator has finite moments exactly of order less than $\beta$. For
$\beta<1$, the profile $F^{-1}(\gamma|x|^2)$ is larger than
$F^{-1}(\gamma'|x|^{2\beta})$ near the origin for every $\gamma'>0$. Nonexistence
for this profile therefore also follows from Corollary~\ref{cor:datacomp}, but
it is not the dilation-critical profile.
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- remark-15: body_md -->
\rm[Dilation-critical singularity]\label{rem:DCS}
The nonexistence result of Lemma~\ref{L7.3} has a sharp counterpart. Let
$u_c(x)=F^{-1}(\gamma|x|^{2\beta})$ be the untruncated profile. Then there
exists $\gamma^\ast=\gamma^\ast(d,\beta,\varrho,f)$ with
$\gamma_0(d,\beta)\le\gamma^\ast<\infty$ such that \eqref{eq:main} with data
$u_c$ has no nonnegative integral solution for $\gamma<\gamma^\ast$, and has
one, belonging to $L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,T),L^\infty)$, for
$\gamma>\gamma^\ast$. Since $u_c(x/\lambda)=F^{-1}(\gamma\lambda^{-2\beta}
|x|^{2\beta})$, this says that $F^{-1}(\gamma^\ast|x|^{2\beta})$ is a
\emph{dilation-critical singularity} for \eqref{eq:main} in the sense of
\cite[Definition 1.2]{Fujishima2024}. The proof combines the existence part of
\cite{Fujishima2024} with Lemma~\ref{lem:freecomp}, as follows.

We first check that $f$, restricted to $[0,\infty)$, satisfies the hypotheses
of \cite{Fujishima2024} with $\theta=2\beta\in(0,2]$, which is admissible
since \cite{Fujishima2024} covers $0<\theta\le2$. By $(\mathrm C)$, $f$ is
continuous, positive on $(0,\infty)$ and increasing on $[0,\infty)$, which is
condition (M) there. By $(\mathrm C)$ and $(\mathrm G)$, $f\in C^1$ and
$F(u)<\infty$ for $u>0$, which gives the superlinearity condition (S), and
$f$ is convex on $[0,\infty)$, which gives (C). Finally, $(\mathrm G)$ and
\eqref{eq:limitone} show that $q_f:=\lim_{u\to\infty}f'(u)F(u)$ exists and
equals $1$, which is (L). The relation $1/p_f+1/q_f=1$ then gives
$p_f=\infty>p_\theta=1+\theta/d$, so $f$ is supercritical in the sense of
\cite{Fujishima2024}.

Next, \cite[Theorem 1.1(b)]{Fujishima2024} asserts the following. If $\mu$ is
nonnegative and bounded away from the origin, and if
$1/F(\mu(x))\le\varepsilon|x|^{-\theta}$ near the origin for a suitable
$\varepsilon=\varepsilon(d,\theta,f)>0$, then the free problem
$\partial_tu+(-\Delta)^{\theta/2}u=f(u)$ possesses, for some $T>0$, a solution
$U\in L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,T),L^\infty)$. Here a solution is a
nonnegative, measurable, almost everywhere finite function satisfying the
Duhamel formula with the kernel of $e^{-t(-\Delta)^{\theta/2}}$, which is
$p_\beta$ for $\theta=2\beta$; this is exactly a nonnegative integral solution
of the free problem in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:solutions}(i). The
data $u_c$ is bounded away from the origin and satisfies
$1/F(u_c(x))=\gamma^{-1}|x|^{-2\beta}$. Hence, for $\gamma\ge1/\varepsilon$,
the free problem has such a solution $U$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:freecomp}
provides a nonnegative integral solution $u\le U$ of \eqref{eq:main} with data
$u_c$; in particular $u\in L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}((0,T),L^\infty)$.

Finally, let $\gamma^\ast$ be the infimum of all $\gamma>0$ for which
\eqref{eq:main} with data $u_c$ has a nonnegative integral solution. Since
$F^{-1}$ is decreasing, $u_c$ decreases as $\gamma$ increases, and
Corollary~\ref{cor:datacomp} shows that solvability for some $\gamma$ implies
solvability for every larger $\gamma$. By the previous step,
$\gamma^\ast\le1/\varepsilon<\infty$, and by Lemma~\ref{L7.3},
$\gamma^\ast\ge\gamma_0(d,\beta)$. This proves the claim. The same
monotonicity argument applies to the truncated data \eqref{eq:data}.
<!-- end remark-15 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:RV}
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$ and $(\mathrm G)$. Then $\log f$ is regularly
varying of index $\rho$, $f^{k}\approx f$ for every $k>0$, $u^{-k}f(u)\to\infty$
for every $k>0$, $\lim_{u\to\infty}f'(u)F(u)=1$, and $fF$ is regularly varying
of index $1-\rho\in[-\infty,1)$. Consequently, for $\varepsilon,\lambda\in(0,1)$
and $M>0$ there exist $y_1,y_2,y_3>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:RV1}
1-\varepsilon\le f'(y)F(y)\le1+\varepsilon,\qquad y\ge y_1,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:RV2}
\frac{f(\lambda y)F(\lambda y)}{\lambda f(y)F(y)}\ \ge\
\begin{cases}
\lambda^{-\rho}-\varepsilon, & \rho<\infty,\ y\ge y_2,\\[3pt]
M, & \rho=\infty,\ y\ge y_3 .
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:ICinf}
Let $f$ satisfy $(\mathrm C)$ and $(\mathrm G)$, and let $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ with
$\Phi\gtrsim f$ at infinity. Then there is $\lambda_1>0$ such that
$\int_1^\infty x^{-p_\ast}f'\bigl(\lambda_1\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x<\infty$.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
The function $f$ is a strictly increasing bijection of $[0,\infty)$, and
$\Phi\gtrsim f$ gives $K,y_0>0$ with $f(y)\le\Phi(Ky)$ for $y\ge y_0$. Since
$\Phi$ is a bijection, we infer that $\Phi^{-1}(x)\le Kf^{-1}(x)$ for
$x\ge f(y_0)$. Let
$\bar\lambda\in(0,1)$ and $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ be fixed below and put
$\lambda_1:=\bar\lambda/K$. For $a$ large, substituting $x=f(y)$ and using the
right inequality in \eqref{eq:RV1} at $y$ and at $\bar\lambda y$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:C1}
\int_a^{\infty}x^{-p_\ast}f'\bigl(\lambda_1\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x
\le\int_b^{\infty}f(y)^{-p_\ast}f'(\bar\lambda y)f'(y)\,\mathrm{d} y
\le(1+\varepsilon)^2\int_b^{\infty}h(y)\,\mathrm{d} y ,
\end{equation}
where $b=f^{-1}(a)$ and $h(y):=\bigl[f(y)^{p_\ast}F(y)F(\bar\lambda y)\bigr]^{-1}$.
Using $F'=-1/f$ and the left inequality in \eqref{eq:RV1}, we compute
\begin{align*}
h'(y)&=-h(y)\Bigl(p_\ast\frac{f'(y)}{f(y)}-\frac{1}{f(y)F(y)}
-\frac{\bar\lambda}{f(\bar\lambda y)F(\bar\lambda y)}\Bigr)\\
&\le-\frac{\bar\lambda h(y)}{f(\bar\lambda y)F(\bar\lambda y)}
\Bigl(\bigl(\tfrac{2\beta}{d}-\varepsilon p_\ast\bigr)
\frac{f(\bar\lambda y)F(\bar\lambda y)}{\bar\lambda f(y)F(y)}-1\Bigr),
\end{align*}
since $p_\ast(1-\varepsilon)-1=\tfrac{2\beta}{d}-\varepsilon p_\ast$. Fix
$M>d/(2\beta)$, let $0<\varepsilon<2\beta/(dp_\ast)$, and set
$$
\alpha_1(\rho):=
\begin{cases}
\bigl(\tfrac{2\beta}{d}-\varepsilon p_\ast\bigr)
(\bar\lambda^{-\rho}-\varepsilon)-1, & \rho\in(0,\infty),\\[4pt]
\bigl(\tfrac{2\beta}{d}-\varepsilon p_\ast\bigr)M-1, & \rho=\infty .
\end{cases}
$$
For $\rho<\infty$, we choose $\bar\lambda\in(0,1)$ so small that
$\alpha_1(\rho)>0$. For $\rho=\infty$, we choose $\varepsilon$ so small that
$\alpha_1(\rho)>0$, and $\bar\lambda\in(0,1)$ arbitrary. Then \eqref{eq:RV2}
and the right inequality in \eqref{eq:RV1} give, for $y$ large,
\begin{align*}
h'(y)&\le-\frac{\alpha_1(\rho)\bar\lambda h(y)}{f(\bar\lambda y)F(\bar\lambda y)}
\le-\frac{\alpha_1(\rho)}{1+\varepsilon}\,
\bar\lambda\frac{f'(\bar\lambda y)}{f(\bar\lambda y)}\,h(y)\\
&=-A_1\bigl(\log f(\bar\lambda y)\bigr)'h(y),
\qquad A_1:=\frac{\alpha_1(\rho)}{1+\varepsilon}>0 ,
\end{align*}
and integration yields $h(y)\le C[f(\bar\lambda y)]^{-A_1}$ for $y$ large. Since
$f$ grows faster than any polynomial, $h$ is integrable at infinity, and we
conclude that \eqref{eq:C1} is finite. On $[1,a]$, the integrand is bounded.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Let $\lambda_1$ be as in Lemma~\ref{lem:ICinf}. By $(\Lambda)$,
$$
\ell\bigl(\lambda_1\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)\le Cf'\bigl(\lambda_1\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)
\qquad\text{whenever }\lambda_1\Phi^{-1}(x)>\kappa_0,
$$
that is, for $x$ large. On the remaining bounded interval, the integrand in
\eqref{eq:IC-infty} is bounded. Hence \eqref{eq:IC-infty} holds with
$\lambda=\lambda_1$, and Theorem~\ref{thm:local} applies.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
We follow \cite[Section 6.2]{Laister}. First, there are $C_1,\mu>0$ with
$f(u)^r\le C_1\Phi(\mu u)$ for all $u\ge0$. Near $0$, this is the hypothesis
$\Phi\gtrsim f^r$. At infinity, it follows from $f^r\approx f$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:RV}) and $\Phi\gtrsim f$, by transitivity of $\lesssim$. On
the remaining compact interval $[u_1,u_2]\subset(0,\infty)$, the quotient
$f(u)^r/\Phi(\mu u)$ is bounded, because $\Phi$ is positive and increasing.

Let $v$ be an $M_\Phi$-mild solution on $[0,T)$ with data $u_0$, and fix
$T_1<T$. The set $\mathcal V:=v([0,T_1])$ is compact in $M_\Phi$, so there is
$A_{\mathcal V}<\infty$ with $\int\Phi(4\mu|\psi|)\le A_{\mathcal V}$ for
$\psi\in\mathcal V$ (cover $\mathcal V$ by finitely many balls of radius
$1/(8\mu)$ and use convexity). By uniform continuity, there is $\tau_0>0$ with
$\|v(s')-v(s)\|_\Phi\le1/(4\mu)$ whenever $s,s'\in[0,T_1]$, $|s-s'|\le\tau_0$.
For such $s\le s'$, the convexity of $\Phi$ gives
\begin{align*}
\|f(v(s'))\|_{L^r}^r&\le C^r\int f(|v(s')|)^r
\le C^rC_1\int\Phi(\mu|v(s')|)\\
&\le\frac{C^rC_1}{2}\int\bigl[\Phi(2\mu|v(s')-v(s)|)+\Phi(2\mu|v(s)|)\bigr]
\le C^rC_1\bigl(1+A_{\mathcal V}\bigr).
\end{align*}
Let $s\in[0,T_1)$ be such that Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}(i) applies (or $s=0$).
For $0<t\le\min\{\tau_0,T_1-s\}$,
$v(s+t)=S_\beta(t)v(s)+\int_0^tS_\beta(t-\sigma)f(v(s+\sigma))\,\mathrm{d}\sigma$, and by
Lemma~\ref{lem:minkowski} and Proposition~\ref{prop:LpLq} (with the pair of
exponents $(r,\infty)$),
$$
\Bigl\|\int_0^tS_\beta(t-\sigma)f(v(s+\sigma))\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\Bigr\|_\infty
\le C\int_0^t(t-\sigma)^{-\frac{d}{2\beta r}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\
\sup_{s\le s'\le s+\tau_0}\|f(v(s'))\|_{L^r}
\le C\,\tau_0^{\,1-\frac{d}{2\beta r}} ,
$$
since $r>d/(2\beta)$, while $\|S_\beta(t)v(s)\|_\infty\le
C_\infty\alpha(t)\|v(s)\|_\Phi$. Hence $v$ is bounded on $\mathbb R^d\times[s+\epsilon,
s+\tau_0]$ for every $\epsilon>0$. Since the admissible $s$ are dense, we infer
that $v\in L^\infty_{\rm loc}((0,T_1),L^\infty)$, and $T_1<T$ was arbitrary.
Thus $v$ is a bounded $M_\Phi$-mild solution, and Theorem~\ref{thm:local}(b)
shows that $v=u(\cdot;u_0)$ and $T\le T_{u_0}$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Let $\gamma\in(0,\gamma_0(d,\beta))$ and let $u_0$ be the initial data
\eqref{eq:data}, with $R>0$ to be fixed. By Lemma \ref{L7.1}, problem
\eqref{eq:main} has no nonnegative integral solution with this data. It only
remains to check that $u_0\in L_\Phi$ for a suitable choice of $R$.

Since $\Phi\lesssim f$, there are $K,u_1>0$ with $\Phi(u)\le f(Ku)$ for
$u\ge u_1$. Let $\lambda\in(0,1)$ be fixed below, put $\lambda_0:=\lambda/K$,
and choose $R>0$ so small that $\lambda_0F^{-1}(\gamma R^{2\beta})\ge u_1$,
which is possible by \eqref{eq:Finv}. As $u_0$ is radially decreasing, we have
$\lambda_0u_0(x)\ge u_1$ on $B_R\setminus\{0\}$, and therefore
\begin{align*}
\int_{\mathbb R^d}\Phi\bigl(\lambda_0u_0\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x
&\le\int_{B_R}f\bigl(\lambda F^{-1}(\gamma|x|^{2\beta})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x
=|\mathbb S^{d-1}|\int_0^{R}f\bigl(\lambda F^{-1}(\gamma r^{2\beta})\bigr)r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d} r\\
&=\frac{|\mathbb S^{d-1}|}{2\beta\gamma^{d/(2\beta)}}
\int_b^{\infty}\frac{f(\lambda s)}{f(s)}\bigl[F(s)\bigr]^{\nu}\,\mathrm{d} s ,
\end{align*}
with $\nu:=\frac{d}{2\beta}-1$ and $b:=F^{-1}(\gamma R^{2\beta})$, where we
substituted $\gamma r^{2\beta}=F(s)$ and used $F'=-1/f$. Writing
$g(s):=f(\lambda s)f(s)^{-1}[F(s)]^{\nu}$ and using \eqref{eq:RV1}, we compute
\begin{align*}
g'(s)&=-g(s)\Bigl(\frac{f'(s)}{f(s)}-\lambda\frac{f'(\lambda s)}{f(\lambda s)}
+\frac{\nu}{f(s)F(s)}\Bigr)\\
&\le-\frac{\lambda g(s)}{f(\lambda s)F(\lambda s)}
\Bigl(\bigl(\tfrac{d}{2\beta}-\varepsilon\bigr)
\frac{f(\lambda s)F(\lambda s)}{\lambda f(s)F(s)}-(1+\varepsilon)\Bigr),
\end{align*}
the constant $\tfrac{d}{2\beta}-\varepsilon$ arising as $1-\varepsilon+\nu$.
Let $M>0$ and $\varepsilon\in(0,\min\{1,d/(2\beta)\})$, and set
$$
\alpha_2(\rho):=
\begin{cases}
\bigl(\tfrac{d}{2\beta}-\varepsilon\bigr)(\lambda^{-\rho}-\varepsilon)
-(1+\varepsilon), & \rho\in(0,\infty),\\[4pt]
\bigl(\tfrac{d}{2\beta}-\varepsilon\bigr)M-(1+\varepsilon), & \rho=\infty .
\end{cases}
$$
For $\rho<\infty$, we choose $\lambda\in(0,1)$ small; for $\rho=\infty$, we
choose $M$ large and $\lambda\in(0,1)$ arbitrary. In both cases
$\alpha_2(\rho)>0$. Then, exactly as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:ICinf}, we
get $g(s)\le C[f(\lambda s)]^{-A_2}$ for $s$ large, with
$A_2=\alpha_2(\rho)/(1+\varepsilon)>0$. Since $f$ grows faster than any
polynomial, $g$ is integrable at infinity. We conclude that
$\int\Phi(\lambda_0u_0)\,\mathrm{d} x<\infty$, that is, $u_0\in L_\Phi$.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:nu}
The computation above is valid for every $d\ge1$ and $\beta\in(0,1]$, since only
the positivity of $1-\varepsilon+\nu=\frac{d}{2\beta}-\varepsilon$ is used. In
particular, no separate treatment is needed when $\nu<0$, which happens only
for $d=1$ and $\beta\in(\frac12,1]$. The local integrability of $u_0$ required
by Lemma~\ref{L7.2} was established in Lemma~\ref{L7.1}.
<!-- end remark-16 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
\rm\label{rem:notinM}
Assume that $f$ also satisfies $(\Lambda)$ and that $\Phi\in\mathcal{N}$ satisfies
$\Phi\approx f$ at infinity. Then the initial data $u_0$ constructed above lies in
$L_\Phi\setminus M_\Phi$. Otherwise, Theorem~\ref{thm:critical}(a)(i) and
Theorem~\ref{thm:local}(a) would provide a nonnegative (since $f$ is
positone) bounded $M_\Phi$-mild, hence integral, solution. In particular,
$M_\Phi\neq L_\Phi$, which is consistent with the fact that $\Phi$ cannot satisfy the
$\Delta_2$-condition when $\Phi\approx f$ is of faster than polynomial growth.
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Since $(\Lambda_0)$ implies $(\Lambda)$, part (a) gives \eqref{eq:IC-infty}
and the conclusions of Theorem~\ref{thm:local}. For the global statement, we
must produce $\lambda>0$ with
$$I_0=\frac{2\beta}{d}\int_0^\infty x^{-p_\ast}\ell(\lambda\Phi^{-1}(x))\,\mathrm{d} x<1.$$
By $(\Lambda_0)$, it suffices to show that $\int_0^{\infty}H(\lambda,x)\,\mathrm{d} x\to0$
as $\lambda\to0$, where $H(\lambda,x):=x^{-p_\ast}f'\bigl(\lambda\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)$.
Let $\lambda_1$ be as in Lemma~\ref{lem:ICinf} and
$\lambda':=\min\{\lambda_0,\lambda_1\}$. Since $f'$ is increasing, $0\le
H(\lambda,x)\le H(\lambda',x)$ for $\lambda\le\lambda'$, and $H(\lambda',\cdot)$
is integrable on $(0,1)$ by \eqref{eq:C-b} and on $(1,\infty)$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:ICinf}. Since $f'$ is continuous with $f'(0)=0$, we have
$H(\lambda,x)\to0$ pointwise as $\lambda\to0$, and dominated convergence gives
$\int_0^\infty H(\lambda,x)\,\mathrm{d} x\to0$. Hence \eqref{eq:IC-zero} holds for all
sufficiently small $\lambda\in(0,\lambda']$, and Corollary \ref{thm:global}
applies.

For Corollary \ref{cor:C}, it remains to verify \eqref{eq:C-b}. As $f'$ is
regularly varying at zero of index $m-1$, we may write
$f'(x)=x^{m-1}\mu(1/x)$ for $x\in(0,1)$ with $\mu$ slowly varying at infinity;
in particular $f'(0)=0$, because $m>p_\ast>1$. From $\Phi(u)\gtrsim u^{q}$ near
zero and the bijectivity of $\Phi$, we get $\Phi^{-1}(x)\le Kx^{1/q}$ for
$x\in(0,a)$. Hence, with $\lambda=\lambda_0K$ and $b=a^{1/q}$,
\begin{align*}
\int_0^{a}x^{-p_\ast}f'\bigl(\lambda_0\Phi^{-1}(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x
&\le\int_0^{a}x^{-p_\ast}f'\bigl(\lambda x^{1/q}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} x
=q\int_0^{b}y^{\,-1-\frac{2\beta q}{d}}f'(\lambda y)\,\mathrm{d} y\\
&=q\lambda^{m-1}\int_0^{b}y^{\,m-2-\frac{2\beta q}{d}}\mu\Bigl(\frac{1}{\lambda y}\Bigr)
\,\mathrm{d} y\\
&=q\lambda^{m-1}\int_{1/b}^{\infty}z^{-m+\frac{2\beta q}{d}}\,
\mu\Bigl(\frac z\lambda\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d} z ,
\end{align*}
after the substitutions $x=y^{q}$ and $y=1/z$ (we assume $\lambda b<1$, which
holds for $\lambda_0$ small). The last integral converges because
$z\mapsto\mu(z/\lambda)$ is slowly varying and $-m+2\beta q/d<-1$, which is
exactly the hypothesis $q<\frac{d(m-1)}{2\beta}$; see
\cite[Proposition 1.5.10]{BGT}. On $(a,1)$, the integrand in \eqref{eq:C-b} is
bounded.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Theorem~\ref{thm:critical}(a)(i) gives \eqref{eq:IC-infty} and the conclusions
of Theorem~\ref{thm:local}. By $(\Lambda_0)$, $\ell(r)\le Cf'(r)$ for $r>0$.
Hence \eqref{eq:dini} holds, and Theorem~\ref{thm:confined} applies. If $f'$ is
regularly varying at zero of index $m-1>0$, then
$f'(r)=r^{m-1}\mu(1/r)\le r^{(m-1)/2}$ for small $r$, and the Dini condition
holds.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
